Structural Parameterization of Steiner Tree Packing
Niko Hastrich, Kirill Simonov

Abstract
Steiner Tree Packing ( 𝖲𝖳𝖯 ) (\stp) is a notoriously hard problem in classical complexity theory, which is of practical relevance to VLSI circuit design. Previous research has approached this problem by providing heuristic or approximate algorithms. In this paper, we show the first 𝖥𝖯𝖳 \fpt algorithms for 𝖲𝖳𝖯 \stp parameterized by structural parameters of the input graph. In particular, we show that 𝖲𝖳𝖯 \stp is fixed-parameter tractable by the tree-cut width as well as the fracture number of the input graph. To achieve our results, we generalize techniques from Edge-Disjoint Paths ( 𝖤𝖣𝖯 \edp ) to Generalized Steiner Tree Packing ( 𝖦𝖲𝖳𝖯 \gstp ), which generalizes both 𝖲𝖳𝖯 \stp and 𝖤𝖣𝖯 \edp . First, we derive the notion of the augmented graph for 𝖦𝖲𝖳𝖯 \gstp analogous to 𝖤𝖣𝖯 \edp . We then show that 𝖦𝖲𝖳𝖯 \gstp is 𝖥𝖯𝖳 \fpt by • the tree-cut width of the augmented graph, • the fracture number of the augmented graph, • the slim tree-cut width of the input graph. The latter two results were previously known for 𝖤𝖣𝖯 \edp ; our results generalize these to 𝖦𝖲𝖳𝖯 \gstp and improve the running time for the parameter fracture number. On the other hand, it was open whether 𝖤𝖣𝖯 \edp is 𝖥𝖯𝖳 \fpt parameterized by the tree-cut width of the augmented graph, despite extensive research on the structural complexity of the problem. We settle this question affirmatively.

1
Introduction

In
Steiner tree packing
(
\stp
), we are given a triple
(G,T,d)
, where
G
is a graph,
T\subseteq V(G)
is the
terminal set
, and
d\in\N^{+}
is the
demand
.
The goal is to decide whether there are
d
edge-disjoint trees
F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{d}
in
G
which all include the vertices
T
.
We can see that this problem is polynomial time solvable if
{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\leq 2
or
d=1
.
However, if
{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\geq 3
or
d\geq 2
, this problem becomes
\nphard

[
Kaski04
,
AazamiCJ12
]
.
So, unless
\p=\np
, there is no algorithm deciding each instance correctly in polynomial time.
Therefore, in order to give a polynomial time algorithm, we have to resort to heuristic solutions or abandon the goal of solving each instance.

Despite this complexity,
\stp
—and the related problems
\edp
and
\gstp
, which we introduce shortly—has applications in practical fields like VLSI circuit design
[
Luk85
,
BursteinP83
,
CohoonH88
,
MartinW93
,
Pulleyblank95
,
GrotschelMW97
]
.
Here, the edges represent wires and the terminals represent endpoints that need to be connected.
Additionally, this problem finds applications in designing computer networks for multicasting
[
OfekY97
,
ChenGY98
,
GargKKP03
]
, which in turn has applications for video-conferencing
[
Zhao+14
]
.
Given this problem’s significance, efficient algorithms are of high interest.
These are typically based on heuristics
[
Luk85
,
BursteinP83
,
CohoonH88
,
OfekY97
,
ChenGY98
]
or integer programming
[
GrotschelMW97
,
ChenGY98
]
as opposed to exploiting structural properties of specific instances.
These approaches typically provide no guarantees on the quality of their output compared to an optimal solution.

For
\stp
, there are also algorithms known, which provide such guarantees.
If there are
d
edge-disjoint subgraphs connecting
T
, we know that
T
is
d
-edge-connected (i.e., we need to remove at least
d
edges to disconnect
T
).
Kriesell
[
Kriesell03
]
proved that for each
n\in\N
there is a function
f_{n}\colon\N\to\N
such that if
{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\leq n
and
T
is
f_{n}(d)
-edge-connected, then there are
d
edge-disjoint subgraphs connecting
T
in
G
.
In the same paper, Kriesell conjectured that these
f_{n}
are bounded by a single function.
Concretely, he conjectured that if
T
is
2d
-edge-connected, then
G
contains
d
edge-disjoint trees connecting
T
.

This conjecture is known to be true for many special cases.
As an example, this conjecture has long been known to be true for
T=V(G)

[
NashWilliams61
,
Tutte61
]
.
This means, if
G
is
2d
-edge-connected, it has at least
d
edge-disjoint spanning trees.
On the other end of the size of
T
, we know that this conjecture is true for
{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\leq 5

[
JainMS03
]
.
Additionally, the conjecture is known to be correct if
G
is Eulerian
[
Kriesell03
]
.

The first result proving, without additional assumptions on
G,d
and
T
, that there is a constant
c\in\R
such that if
T
is
cd
-edge-connected, there are
d
edge-disjoint Steiner trees in
G
, is due to Lau
[
Lau04
]
, who proved that
c=26
is a valid choice.
This was later improved to
c=6.5

[
WestW12
]
.
The state-of-the-art result is that there are
d
edge-disjoint Steiner trees, if
T
is
(5d+4)
-edge-connected
[
DeVosMP16
]
.
These results are all obtained by providing polynomial time algorithms that given a
cd
-edge-connected graph, output
d
edge-disjoint Steiner trees.
So, the last result can be seen as a
\frac{1}{5}-{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(1/\mathrm{OPT}\right)}{\OOp(1/\mathrm{OPT})}{\OOp(1/\mathrm{OPT})}{\OOp(1/\mathrm{OPT})}}
approximation algorithm.

Still, none of these algorithms provide exact solutions in polynomial time.
In this paper, we approach this problem from the other direction.
We significantly broaden the class of instances for which there is a known polynomial time algorithm.
We achieve this by exploiting inherent structure that exists in some instances.
More concretely, we study
\stp
parameterized by structural properties and develop algorithms running in
\fpt
-time.

In this paradigm of algorithm design, we consider instances where the underlying graphs exhibit some additional structure.
This structure is captured by an additional number
k\in\N
—the parameter—that is provided to algorithms as input.
Now, we try to find algorithms that are able to exploit this structure.
Ideally, there is a constant
c\in\N
such that the algorithm can decide each instance of size
n
in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(n^{c}\right)}{\OOp(n^{c})}{\OOp(n^{c})}{\OOp(n^{c})}}
.
Roughly speaking, if such an algorithm exists, we call a problem fixed-parameter tractable (
\fpt
) by the considered parameter.

An easy example of a structural parameter is the size of the smallest vertex cover.
Assume we know that there is a vertex cover
S
of size
k\in\N
in our graph.
An example of a problem, where we can exploit this structure is
Clique
.
In this problem, we are given a graph
G
, an integer
\ell\in\N
, and we need to decide whether there is a clique of size
\ell
in
G
.
Now, consider any clique
C
in
G
.
Notice that it contains at most one vertex of
V(G)\setminus S
.
We now iterate through all
X\subseteq S
and search for the largest clique that contains
X
and at most one additional vertex of
V(G)\setminus S
.
For each of the
2^{k}
different choices for
X
, this can be done in
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}+k^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}+k^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}+k^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}+k^{2})}}\leq{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}\right)}{\OOp(k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}}
.
Thus, given
S
, we can compute the largest clique in
G
in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}\right)}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}}
.
Using the fact that we can compute
S
in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}\right)}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(2^{k}k{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}}
given
k

[
Mehlhorn84a
]
, we can solve
Clique
in linear time for every
k
.
Thus,
Clique
is
\fpt
by the size of the smallest vertex cover of
G
.

Before this paper, there was no known
\fpt
algorithm parameterized by structural parameters for
\stp
.
In fact for one of the most widely used structural parameter—treewidth—this is unlikely.
This can be seen, as
\stp
has
Integer 2-Commodity Flow
as a special case
[
AazamiCJ12
]
and this problem has recently been shown to be
\wonehard
parameterized by treewidth
[
BodlaenderMOPL23
]
.
Therefore,
\stp
is
\wonehard
parameterized by treewidth, and it is believed
[
CyganFKLMPPS15
, Chapter 13]
that there is no
\fpt
algorithm for a parameterized problem that is
\wonehard
.
To this date, the only known
\fpt
algorithm for
\stp
is due to Robertson and Seymour
[
RobertsonS95b
]
, showing that
\stp
is fixed-parameter tractable parameterized by
{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}+d
.

To develop
\fpt
algorithms for
\stp
parameterized by structural parameters, we consider the closely related
Edge-Disjoint Paths
(
\edp
) problem.
An
\edp
instance is a tuple
(G,\mathcal{T})
, where
G
is a graph and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
is a set of terminal pairs in
V(G)
.
The task now is to decide if for each
\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{T}
there is an
uv
-path
P_{uv}
in
G
such that all
\{P_{uv}\}_{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{T}}
are pairwise edge-disjoint.
This problem is famously
\fpt
with respect to
{\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{T}\right|}{{|\mathcal{T}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}|}}}

[
RobertsonS95b
]
.
However, it is notoriously hard with respect to classical structural parameters like treewidth.
In fact, it is even
\nphard
on complete bipartite graphs where one bipartition contains 3 vertices
[
Fleszar2018Sep
]
.
These graphs have vertex cover number at most 3, which rules out an
\fpt
algorithm for
\edp
parameterized by many classical structural parameters—like treewidth, fracture number, size of the smallest feedback vertex set, size of the smallest vertex cover—unless
\p=\np
.

However, there are some
\fpt
algorithms known for
\edp
.
They fall roughly in two categories.
The first category are
\fpt
algorithms with respect to structural parameters that are based on edge-cuts like slim tree-cut width, rather than vertex-cuts like treewidth
[
GanianOR20
,
GanianK22
,
BrandCGHK22
,
GanianOR21
]
.
The second category of
\fpt
algorithms based on structural parameters, do not consider the parameter with respect to the host graph
G
, but rather the augmented graph
G+\mathcal{T}
, which is the graph
G
with an edge inserted among every terminal pair
[
GanianOR21
]
.
This helps to take into account where in the graph the terminal pairs are located and how much they “influence” each other.
The problem with transferring these results directly to
\stp
is that although the
\edp
and the
\stp
problem are closely related, there is no simple reduction known between instances of one problem to the other.

However, both are special cases of
Generalized Steiner Tree Packing
(
\gstp
), which is just
\stp
with multiple different terminal sets.
Formally, we are given a triple
(G,\mathcal{T},d)
, where
G
is the underlying graph,
\mathcal{T}\subseteq 2^{V(G)}
is the set of terminal sets, and
d\colon\mathcal{T}\to\N^{+}
gives the demand for each terminal set.
Our task is to decide whether there is a set of pairwise edge-disjoint, connected subgraphs
\mathcal{F}
of
G
and an assignment function
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{T}
.
This assignment function needs to satisfy that every solution subgraph
F\in\mathcal{F}
is assigned to a terminal set
\pi(F)
, which is contained in
F
(i.e.,
\pi(F)\subseteq V(F)
).
Additionally, for every terminal set
T\in\mathcal{T}
, the assignment function needs to assign
d(T)
many solution subgraphs to
T
.

In this paper, we generalize all known
\fpt
algorithms for
\edp
parameterized by structural parameters to
\gstp
, which makes them much more widely applicable.
Firstly, this allows us to apply them to
\stp
.
Secondly, in doing so, we also discover new
\fpt
algorithms for
\edp
, which positively settles the open question, whether
\edp
is
\fpt
with respect to the tree-cut width of the augmented graph in the affirmative.
Finally, we also improve the running time of many known
\fpt
algorithms for
\edp
.
See an overview of our results in comparison to known results in
Table

1
.

The parameterized complexity of
\stp
,

, and

with respect to structural parameters.

D
is the sum of demands.

.
For terminology of the parameters we refer to
Section

2
.
We abbreviate -hard with -h.

This paper is structured as follows.
We start in
Section

2
by introducing relevant definitions and notation.
In
Section

3
the notion of augmentation from
\edp
to
\gstp
.
To the best of our knowledge, there are no known results regarding augmentation of problems, where the terminals are arbitrary sets and not pairs like in
\edp
.
For this, we compare two different approaches and finally settle on one of them which will be used for the remainder of this paper.

Building on this definition, we show in
that
\gstp
is
\fpt
by the fracture number of the augmented graph; due to the way we define augmentation for
\gstp
, this result directly applies to
\stp
as well.
This is in stark contrast to the fact, that
\edp
, and therefore
\gstp
, is
\pnphard
by the fracture number of the host graph
[
Fleszar2018Sep
]
.
The running time we obtain is doubly exponential in the parameter.
This improves upon the triply exponential running time obtained by Ganian et al.
[
GanianOR21
]
for
\edp
parameterized by the fracture number of the augmented graph.

Following this result, we focus on results using tree-cut decompositions in
.
First, we define an additional property for tree-cut decompositions, which we call being
simple
.
Then, we show how to decide an instance of
\gstp
given a simple tree-cut decomposition.
For tree-cut decompositions, we are interested in two measures of the decomposition, its width and slim width, where the former measure is bounded by a function of the latter.
We then show how the last result can be applied to obtain an
\fpt
algorithm parameterized by the slim tree-cut width of the host graph and the tree-cut width of the augmented graph.
This directly implies that
\edp
is
\fpt
by the tree-cut width of the augmented graph, which was not known before.
As
\edp
is
\wonehard
parameterized by the tree-cut width of the host graph, this result can not be extended to find an
\fpt
algorithm parameterized by the tree-cut width of the host graph, unless
\fpt=\wone
, which is believed to be false
[
CyganFKLMPPS15
, Chapter 13]
.

As augmentation—even of a single terminal set—can increase the tree-cut width arbitrarily, the previous result does not directly translate to an
\fpt
algorithm for
\stp
, in contrast to the case with fracture number.
In
, we examine this further.
We show that we can avoid this hurdle and provide an
\fpt
algorithm for
\stp
parameterized by tree-cut width of the host graph.
This is the central result of this paper and combines most of the other results obtained.
To achieve this, our
\fpt
algorithm distinguishes two cases.
If the tree-cut width does not increase by too much due to augmentation, we apply the result obtained in
.
Otherwise, we prove that we can discard instances where the demand is not small.
As the tree-cut width can be used to put an upper bound on the treewidth and treewidth is a more general parameter, we then develop an
\fpt
algorithm for
\gstp
parameterized by the sum of treewidth and demand.
This
\fpt
algorithm can be used to solve the case remaining to solve
\stp
parameterized by tree-cut width.
Additionally, this algorithm directly translates to an
\fpt
algorithm for
\edp
parameterized by the sum of treewidth and number of terminal pairs.
This was known before, but nevertheless we improve upon the known running time
[
ZhouTN00
]
.

We conclude this paper in
by summarizing our work and giving open research questions.

2
Preliminaries

We denote the natural numbers with
\N
and the positive natural numbers with
\N^{+}
.
Analogously we define the real numbers as
\R
and the positive real numbers as
\R^{+}
.
Let
f,g\colon\N\to\R^{+}
be functions.
We write
f={\mathchoice{\OOp\left(g\right)}{\OOp(g)}{\OOp(g)}{\OOp(g)}}
if
\limsup_{n\in\N}\frac{f(n)}{g(n)}\neq\infty
and
f=\Omega(g)
if
\liminf_{n\in\N}\frac{f(n)}{g(n)}>0
.
We also use this notation as part of terms—like
f(n)=2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(g(n)\right)}{\OOp(g(n))}{\OOp(g(n))}{\OOp(g(n))}}
, this means that there is a
g^{\prime}={\mathchoice{\OOp\left(g\right)}{\OOp(g)}{\OOp(g)}{\OOp(g)}}
such that
f(n)={\mathchoice{\OOp\left(2^{g^{\prime}(n)}\right)}{\OOp(2^{g^{\prime}(n)})}{\OOp(2^{g^{\prime}(n)})}{\OOp(2^{g^{\prime}(n)})}}
.

For all
i\in\N
, we define
{\mathchoice{\left[i\right]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}}\coloneqq\{j\in\N\mid j\leq i\}
and
{\mathchoice{\left[i\right]}{[i]}{[i]}{[i]}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\left[i\right]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}{[i]_{0}}}\setminus\{0\}
.
Note that
{\mathchoice{\left[0\right]}{[0]}{[0]}{[0]}}=\emptyset
.
Let
A
be a finite set of size
n\in\N^{+}
.
For all
k\in\N
, we denote the set of subsets of
A
of size
k
with
\binom{A}{k}\coloneqq\{S\subseteq A\mid{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}=k\}
.
Additionally, we denote the number of such subsets of
A
with
\binom{n}{k}\leq n^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(k\right)}{\OOp(k)}{\OOp(k)}{\OOp(k)}}
, as well.
The number of subsets of
A
with size at most
k
is
\sum_{i\leq k}\binom{n}{i}\leq\binom{n+k}{k}
.
For all
k\in\N^{+}
, the set of functions
f\colon A\to\N
with
\sum_{a\in A}f(a)=k
has size
\binom{n+k-1}{k-1}
and can be enumerated in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(n\binom{n+k-1}{k-1}\right)}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k-1}{k-1})}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k-1}{k-1})}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k-1}{k-1})}}
.
The set of functions
f\colon A\to\N
with
\sum_{a\in A}f(a)\leq k
has size
\binom{n+k}{k}
and can be enumerated in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(n\binom{n+k}{k}\right)}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k}{k})}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k}{k})}{\OOp(n\binom{n+k}{k})}}
.
We denote the identify function on
A
with
{\mathchoice{\identityOp\left(A\right)}{\identityOp(A)}{\identityOp(A)}{\identityOp(A)}}
and the powerset of
A
with
2^{A}\coloneqq\{S\}_{S\subseteq A}
.

Let
A,B
be sets and
f\colon A\to B
a function.
We abbreviate the codomain
B
with
{\mathchoice{\codomainOp\left(B\right)}{\codomainOp(B)}{\codomainOp(B)}{\codomainOp(B)}}
.
For every
X\subseteq A
, we refer to
f
restricted to
X
with
{\mathchoice{{f}\big|_{X}}{{f}\big|_{X}}{{f}\big|_{X}}{{f}\big|_{X}}}
, the image of
X
under
f
as
f(X)\coloneqq\{f(x)\}_{x\in X}
and the image of
f
as
{\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(f\right)}{\imageOp(f)}{\imageOp(f)}{\imageOp(f)}}\coloneqq f(A)
.
For
Y\subseteq B
, we refer to the inverse of
Y
under
f
by
f^{-1}(Y)\coloneqq\{a\in A\mid f(a)\in Y\}
.
For all
b\in B
, we abbreviate
f^{-1}(\{b\})
with
f^{-1}(b)
and if
{\mathchoice{\left|f^{-1}(b)\right|}{{|f^{-1}(b)|}}{{|f^{-1}(b)|}}{{|f^{-1}(b)|}}}=1
, we use this as a function where the result is a single value of
A
and not a singleton set of
A
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph.
We refer to its vertices by
V(G)
and its edges by
E(G)
.
Unless stated otherwise, we only consider simple undirected graphs, that is graphs without loops or multiple edges.
We define the size of
G
as
{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}
.
All of these definitions apply to subgraphs as well.
We denote the set of connected components of
G
as
{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G\right)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}}
, where the elements of this set are the maximally connected subgraphs of
G
.
Wherever possible, we refer to vertices or edges by lower case letters, (sub/hyper)graphs by upper case letters, and sets of vertices, edges, or graphs by calligraphic letters.

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
be a vertex set.
We denote the graph vertex induced on
G
by
S
with
G[S]\coloneqq(S,\{uv\in E\mid u,v\in S\})
.
Now, let
S^{\prime}\subseteq E(G)
be an edge set.
We denote the graph edge induced on
G
by
S^{\prime}
with
G[S^{\prime}]\coloneqq(\bigcup_{uv\in S^{\prime}}\{u,v\},S^{\prime})
as all edges in
S^{\prime}
with their incident vertices.
We also use this notation, if
S
or
S^{\prime}
are not necessarily contained in
V(G)
or
E(G)
, respectively.
In these cases, we implicitly refer to
G[S\cap V(G)]
and
G[S^{\prime}\cap E(G)]
, respectively.
Whether we refer to a vertex or edge set follows from context.
We set the difference graphs
G-S\coloneqq G[V(G)\setminus S]
and
G-S^{\prime}\coloneqq G[E(G)\setminus S^{\prime}]
.
Let
H
be another graph.
We denote the union of
G
and
H
with
G\cup H\coloneqq(V(G)\cup V(H),E(G)\cup E(H))
.
For a set of vertices
S
, we write
G+S\coloneqq(V(G)\cup S,E(G))
as the smallest graph that contains
G
and all vertices in
S
; and for a set of edges
S^{\prime}
, we write
G+S^{\prime}\coloneqq\{V(G)\cup\bigcup_{uv\in S^{\prime}}\{u,v\},E(G)\cup S^{\prime}\}
as the smallest graph that contains
G
and all edges in
S^{\prime}
.

Let
uv\in E(G)
.
Subdividing
uv
introduces a new vertex
s_{uv}
into
G
, and connects it to
u
and
v
.
Formally, we obtain the graph
G-uv+\{s_{uv}u,s_{uv}v\}
.
Contracting the edge
uv
, merges
u
and
v
into a single vertex, while keeping vertices adjacent to
u
or
v
adjacent to the combined vertex.
We denote the graph after contracting
uv
by
G/uv\coloneqq G-v+\{ux\}_{vx\in E(G)}
.
Suppressing a vertex
v\in V
is the operation of directly connecting all neighbors of
v
and removing
v
from
G
.
Formally, the graph after suppressing
v
is
G-v+\{xy\mid\{x,y\}\in\binom{N(v)}{2}\}
.

For
v\in V(G)
, we denote with the open neighborhood with
N_{G}(v)\coloneqq\{u\mid uv\in E(G)\}
and the closed neighborhood with
N_{G}[v]\coloneqq N_{G}(v)\cup\{v\}
.
The degree of a vertex is
{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}}={\mathchoice{\left|N_{G}(V)\right|}{{|N_{G}(V)|}}{{|N_{G}(V)|}}{{|N_{G}(V)|}}}
.
For any
S\subseteq V(G)
, we denote the edges not completely contained in
S
or
V(G)\setminus S
by
{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp_{G}\left(S\right)}{\cutEdgesOp_{G}(S)}{\cutEdgesOp_{G}(S)}{\cutEdgesOp_{G}(S)}}
.
We leave off the index, if the graph is clear from context.

We call
G
edgeless, if
E(G)=\emptyset
.
A walk
W
in
G
is a sequence of edges, such that neighboring edges in
W
share a common vertex.
A path
P
is a walk where each vertex appears at most once.
A cycle
C
is a walk
W\coloneqq w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{\ell}
, such that
w_{1}=w_{\ell}
.
We call a cycle simple, if
\ell\geq 3
and
w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{\ell-1}
is a path.
We call
G
cyclefree, if there is no simple cycle contained in
G
.
There is a path between two distinct vertices
u,v\in V(G)
, if and only if for each
S\subseteq V(G)
with
u\in S
and
v\notin S
, we have
{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(S\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}}\neq\emptyset
.
Similarly,
G
is connected if and only if for all
\emptyset\subset S\subset V(G)
, the set
{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(S\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}{\cutEdgesOp(S)}}\neq\emptyset
.

We call
\phi\colon V(G)\to V(H)
a graph homomorphism, is for all
uv\in E(G)
, we have
\phi(u)\phi(v)\in E(H)
.
To capture this fact, we also write
\phi\colon G\to H
.
If
\phi
is a bijection, we call it an isomorphism.
We denote with
\phi_{e}\colon E(G)\to E(H)
the function
uv\mapsto\phi(u)\phi(v)
.

Let
\phi_{v}\colon V(G)\to V(H)
be an injection and
\phi_{e}
be a function from edges
xy\in E(G)
to
\phi_{v}(x)\phi_{v}(y)
-paths in
H
, such that all
{\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(\phi_{e}\right)}{\imageOp(\phi_{e})}{\imageOp(\phi_{e})}{\imageOp(\phi_{e})}}
are edge-disjoint, we call
(\phi_{v},\phi_{e})
an immersion from
G
into
H
.

Now, let
G=(V,\mathcal{E})
be a hypergraph.
We call
G
minimally-connected if for every
E\in\mathcal{E}
, the hypergraph
G-E
is no longer connected.

An instance of
Generalized Steiner Tree Packing
is a triple
\mathscr{P}\coloneqq(G,\mathcal{T},d)
, where
G
is a graph,
\mathcal{T}\subseteq 2^{V(G)}
is the set of terminal sets, and
d\colon\mathcal{T}\to\N^{+}
is the demand for each terminal set.
This instance is positive, if there is a tuple
(\mathcal{F},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{F}
is a set of edge-disjoint, connected subgraphs of
G
and
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{T}
is an assignment function such that for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
\pi(F)\subseteq V(F)
, and for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
{\mathchoice{\left|\pi^{-1}(T)\right|}{{|\pi^{-1}(T)|}}{{|\pi^{-1}(T)|}}{{|\pi^{-1}(T)|}}}=d(T)
.
Note that, if an instance is positive, we can always obtain a solution where every
F\in\mathcal{F}
is a tree.
We define the size of an instance
\mathscr{P}
as
{\mathchoice{\left|\mathscr{P}\right|}{{|\mathscr{P}|}}{{|\mathscr{P}|}}{{|\mathscr{P}|}}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}}+\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}}{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}
.

An instance of
Edge-Disjoint Paths
is a triple
(G,\mathcal{T})
, where
G
is a graph,
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
is the set of terminal pairs.
This instance is positive, if the instance
(G,\mathcal{T},T\mapsto 1)
of
Generalized Steiner Tree Packing
is positive.
An instance of
Steiner Tree Packing
is a triple
(G,T,d)
, where
G
is a graph,
T\subseteq V(G)
are the terminals, and
d\in\N
is the demand.
This instance is positive, if the instance
(G,\{T\},T\mapsto d)
of
Generalized Steiner Tree Packing
is positive.
Since the assignment function of any solution is trivial, we may also refer to a solution by simply
\mathcal{F}
.

In this paper, we use the paradigm of integer linear programs.
Here, we consider a vector of variables
{\bm{v}}\in\N^{n}
where
n\in\N^{+}
is the length of the vector.
We want to maximize or minimize a linear function of
{\bm{v}}
, where
{\bm{v}}
needs to satisfy some linear constraints.
Each constraint can enforce equality, less-than, and greater-than constraints between two affine functions of
{\bm{v}}
.
To check, whether there is a feasible
{\bm{v}}
, we maximize the linear function
{\bm{x}}\mapsto 0
.
It is known that this problem can be solved in time
n^{{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(n\right)}{\OOp(n)}{\OOp(n)}{\OOp(n)}}}

[
Kannan87
]
.

2.1
Parameterized Complexity

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.
We call
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}\times\N
a parameterized problem.
For an instance
(x,k)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\N
, the value
k
is called the parameter.
We call
L

fixed-parameter tractable
if there is an algorithm, a constant
c\in\R^{+}
, and a computable function
f
, such that for all
(x,k)\in\Sigma^{*}\times\N
the algorithm decides
(x,k)\in L
in time at most
f(k){\mathchoice{\left|x\right|}{{|x|}}{{|x|}}{{|x|}}}^{c}

[
CyganFKLMPPS15
, Chapter 1]
.

Consider
Independent Set
parameterized by the solution size.
That is, for a graph
G
and
k\in\N
, we decide whether there is a
S\subseteq V(G)
with
{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}=k
such that
G[S]
is edge-less.
A parameterized problem
L
is called
\wonehard
, if there is are computable functions
f,g
, a constant
c\in\R^{+}
, and an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that outputs for every instance
(G,k)
of independent set parameterized by the solution size in time
f(k){\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}}^{c}
an equivalent instance
\mathcal{A}(G,k)
of
L
, such that the parameter of
\mathcal{A}(G,k)
is at most
g(k)
.
It is commonly believer that no
\wonehard
problem is fixed-parameter tractable
[
CyganFKLMPPS15
, Chapter 13]
.
We call a parameterized problem
\pnphard
, if it is
\nphard
to the language restricted to instances where the parameter is bounded by some constant.

The
exponential time hypothesis
is the claim that there is a
\delta\in\R^{+}
such that any algorithm that decides
3-Sat
takes on instances with
n
variables at least time
\Omega(2^{\delta n})

[
ImpagliazzoP99
]
.

Consider a parameterized problem
(I,k)
.
Let
r\colon\N\times\N\to\N^{+}
be a runtime function.
For a
f\colon\N\to\N
, we write
r({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}},k)={\mathchoice{\OOp^{\ast}\left(f(k)\right)}{\OOp^{\ast}(f(k))}{\OOp^{\ast}(f(k))}{\OOp^{\ast}(f(k))}}
, if there is a polynomial function
p
such that
r({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}},k)={\mathchoice{\OOp\left(f(k)p({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}})\right)}{\OOp(f(k)p({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}}))}{\OOp(f(k)p({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}}))}{\OOp(f(k)p({\mathchoice{\left|I\right|}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}{{|I|}}}))}}
.
That is, the
\OOp^{\ast}
notation hides a polynomial factor in the size of the instance.

2.1.1
Structural Parameters

Figure 1:
An overview of common structural parameters and their relation. We draw an edge from a parameter
\alpha
to a parameter
\beta
, if given
\alpha
we can compute an upper bound on
\beta
. A Hardness result for a parameter gives a similar results for all ancestors; an
\fpt
-algorithm for a parameter gives an
\fpt
-algorithm for all descendants.

In this paper, we are particularly interested in structural parameters.
We list the formal definition of all parameters considered in this paper here.
For an overview of their relation see
Figure

1
.
For all these definitions, let
G
be the considered graph.

Some of the parameters are based on a
decomposition
, which contains a graph
H
on vertex-sets of
G
.
We refer to the vertices of
H
as nodes or bags and to the edges of
H
as links.

Structural Parameters Based on Vertex Cuts

The following parameters are based on vertex separators.
If they are bounded there is a small set of vertices such if we remove these vertices, the considered graph gets disconnected into considerably smaller parts.
What this means exactly varies from parameter to parameter.
However, all of them have in common, that the number of removed edges is not bounded by a function of the parameter.

Vertex Cover Number

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
.
We call
S
a
vertex-cover
if
G-S
is edgeless.
The size of the smallest vertex cover is called the vertex cover number
{\mathchoice{\vcOp\left(G\right)}{\vcOp(G)}{\vcOp(G)}{\vcOp(G)}}
of
G
.
Finding the smallest vertex cover is
\fpt
by its size.

Feedback Vertex Set Number

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
.
We call
S
a
feedback vertex set
if
G-S
is cyclefree.
The size of the smallest feedback vertex set is called the feedback vertex set number
{\mathchoice{\fvsOp\left(G\right)}{\fvsOp(G)}{\fvsOp(G)}{\fvsOp(G)}}
of
G
.
Finding the smallest feedback vertex set is
\fpt
by its size.

Fracture Number

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
.
We call
S
a
fracture modulator
if every component in
G-S
contains at most
{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}
vertices.
The size of the smallest fracture modulator is called the feedback vertex set number
{\mathchoice{\fvsOp\left(G\right)}{\fvsOp(G)}{\fvsOp(G)}{\fvsOp(G)}}
of
G
.
According to
, we can find a smallest fracture modulator in
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left((2k-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}}\right)}{\OOp((2k-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}})}{\OOp((2k-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}})}{\OOp((2k-1)^{k}{\mathchoice{\left|G\right|}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}{{|G|}}})}}
.

Treewidth

Treewidth builds upon the notion of a tree decomposition of
G
.

Definition 1
.

A
tree decomposition
is a pair
(T,\mathcal{X})
, where
T
is a tree and
\{X_{t}\subseteq V(G)\mid t\in V(T)\}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}
is such that

•

\bigcup_{t\in V(T)}X_{t}=V(G)
,

•

for every
uv\in E(G)
, there is a
t\in V(T)
with
\{u,v\}\subseteq X_{t}
,

•

for every
u\in V(G)
, the subgraph induced on
T
by the bags containing
u
(i.e.,
T[\{t\in V(T)\mid u\in X_{t}\}]
) is connected.

We define the width of a tree decomposition
(T,\mathcal{X})
as
\max_{t\in V(T)}{\mathchoice{\left|X_{t}\right|}{{|X_{t}|}}{{|X_{t}|}}{{|X_{t}|}}}-1
and the treewidth
{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}
of
G
as the minimum width of any tree decomposition of
G
.
There is an algorithm that for all
w\in\N
either outputs a tree decomposition of width
5w+4
, or certifies that
{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}>w
.
This algorithm runs in time
2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(w\right)}{\OOp(w)}{\OOp(w)}{\OOp(w)}}{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}

[
BodlaenderDDFLP16
]
.

To better facilitate dynamic programming with tree decomposition, we introduce the notion of a
nice
tree decomposition.
Contrary to normal tree decomposition, in a nice tree decomposition, the tree
T
is rooted at a vertex
r
.
Additionally, for all leaves
\ell\in V(T)
, we have
X_{\ell}=X_{r}=\emptyset
.
Each inner node
t\in V(T)
has one of three types.

•

Introduce Node
This node has exactly one child
c\in V(T)
and there is a
v\in V(G)\setminus X_{c}
such that
X_{t}=X_{c}\cup\{v\}
.

•

Forget Node
This node has exactly one child
c\in V(T)
and there is a
v\in X_{c}
such that
X_{t}=X_{c}\setminus\{v\}
.

•

Join Node
This node has exactly two children
c,c^{\prime}\in V(T)
and
X_{c}=X_{c^{\prime}}=X_{t}
.

Given a tree decomposition of width
w
, we can in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(w^{2}\max({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}},{\mathchoice{\left|V(T)\right|}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}})\right)}{\OOp(w^{2}\max({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}},{\mathchoice{\left|V(T)\right|}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}}))}{\OOp(w^{2}\max({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}},{\mathchoice{\left|V(T)\right|}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}}))}{\OOp(w^{2}\max({\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}},{\mathchoice{\left|V(T)\right|}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}}))}}
compute a nice tree decomposition of width
w
with at most
w{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}
nodes
[
Kloks94
]
.
As a shorthand notation, for each
t\in V(T)
denote with
T_{t}
the subgraph rooted at
t
and let
Y_{t}\coloneqq\bigcup_{t^{\prime}\in T_{t}}X_{t^{\prime}}
be all the vertices introduces at or below
t
.

A
\mathrm{MSO}_{2}
formula for a graph is a logic formula, that has access to the set of vertices and edges and can check, whether they are incident to each other.
Additionally, quantification over vertices and edges and sets thereof are allowed.
There is an algorithm and a computable function
f\colon\N\times\N\to\N
, such that all
\mathrm{MSO}_{2}
formulas of length
\ell
can be decided in time
{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(f(\ell,{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}){\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}\right)}{\OOp(f(\ell,{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}){\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(f(\ell,{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}){\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}{\OOp(f(\ell,{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}){\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}})}}
.
This fact is known as Courcelle’s theorem
[
BoriePT92
]
.

Structural Parameters Based on Edge Cuts

The following parameters are not only based on vertex cuts.
If any of the parameters presented here is small, we can remove a small number of edges to disconnect the graph into substantially smaller parts.
The exact meaning of this depends on the parameter.

Tree-Cut Width

The parameter
tree-cut width
was introduced by Wollan
[
Wollan15
]
.
We orient our notation at the work of Ganian and Korchemna
[
GanianK22
]
.
Let
X
be a set and
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{\ell}\}
be a family of subsets of
X
such that every two elements of
\mathcal{X}
are disjoint and
X=\bigcup_{X_{t}\in\mathcal{X}}X_{t}
.
We call
\mathcal{X}
a
near-partition
of
X
.
Note that
X_{i}=\emptyset
is allowed.

Definition 2
.

A
tree-cut decomposition
of a graph
G
is a tuple
\mathcal{D}\coloneqq(T,\mathcal{X})
, where
T
is a rooted tree and
\{X_{t}\subseteq V(G)\mid t\in V(T)\}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}
a near-partition of
V(G)
.
A set in
\mathcal{X}
is called a
bag
of the tree-cut decomposition.

For a node
t\in V(T)
, we denote
T_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
the sub-tree rooted at
t
and we set
Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{t\in T_{t}}X_{t}
to be the vertices contained in the bags of
T_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
.
The
adhesion

{\mathchoice{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}\left(t\right)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}}
of
t
is defined as
{\mathchoice{\left|{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}}\right|}{{|{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\cutEdgesOp\left(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}\right)}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}{\cutEdgesOp(Y_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})}}|}}}
and the adhesion of the whole tree-cut decomposition is
\max_{t\in V(T)}{\mathchoice{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}\left(t\right)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}}
.

The
torso
of a tree-cut decomposition at node
t
, denoted as
H_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
, is the graph obtained from
G
as follows.
Consider the connected components of
T-t
and for each
C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(T-t\right)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}}
, denote with
Z_{C}\coloneqq\bigcup_{t\in C}X_{t}
.
The torso at
t
is obtained from
G
, by contracting for all
C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(T-t\right)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}}
the sets
Z_{C}
into a single vertex
z_{C}
.
Note that this may create parallel edges and for
{\mathchoice{\left|V(T)\right|}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}{{|V(T)|}}}=1
, the torso of the root is
G
.
The vertices
\{z_{C}\}_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(T-t\right)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}{\compOp(T-t)}}}
are called
peripheral
and the vertices in
X_{t}
are called
core
vertices of
H_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
.
Consider the unique graph
\tilde{H}_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
, obtained from
H_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
, by repeatedly suppressing vertices of degree at most
2
from
V(H_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})\setminus X_{t}
and removing loops.
This graph is called the
3-center
of
H_{t}^{\mathcal{D}}
with respect to
X_{t}
.

Definition 3
.

Let
\alpha
denote the adhesion of the tree-cut decomposition.
The width of the tree-cut decomposition is

\max\{\alpha\}\cup\left\{{\mathchoice{\left|V(\tilde{H}_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})\right|}{{|V(\tilde{H}_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})|}}{{|V(\tilde{H}_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})|}}{{|V(\tilde{H}_{t}^{\mathcal{D}})|}}}\right\}_{t\in V(t)}.

The tree-cut width of
G
, written as
{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(G\right)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}}
, is the smallest width of a tree-cut decomposition.

To better work with tree-cut decompositions, we need some additional notation and prior results.
For a node
t\in V(T)
, we call
t
empty if
X_{t}=\emptyset
and we denote with
{\mathchoice{\childrenOp\left(t\right)}{\childrenOp(t)}{\childrenOp(t)}{\childrenOp(t)}}
the set of children of
t
in
T
.
Additionally, we call
t\in V(T)

thin
if
{\mathchoice{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}\left(t\right)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\adhesionOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}}\leq 2
and
bold
otherwise.
The set of thin children is denoted by
{\mathchoice{\thinChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}\left(t\right)}{\thinChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\thinChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}{\thinChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(t)}}
and the set of bold children as
{\mathchoice{\boldChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}\left(s\right)}{\boldChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(s)}{\boldChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(s)}{\boldChildrenOp_{\mathcal{D}}(s)}}
.
A tree-cut decomposition is
nice
, if for all thin nodes
t
the sets
N_{G}(Y_{t})
and
\bigcup_{s\text{ is a sibling of }t}Y_{s}^{\mathcal{D}}
are disjoint.
Any tree-cut decomposition can be transformed into a nice tree-cut decomposition in cubic time
[
GanianKS22
]
.
In all notation we leave off the tree-cut decomposition, if it is clear from context.

Let
G^{\prime}
be such that there is an immersion from
G^{\prime}
into
G
, then
{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(G^{\prime}\right)}{\tcwOp(G^{\prime})}{\tcwOp(G^{\prime})}{\tcwOp(G^{\prime})}}\leq{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(G\right)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}}

[
Wollan15
]
.
For all
n,k\in\N^{+}
, consider the graph with a center vertex
c
and outer
n
vertices, each connected to
c
with
k
parallel edges.
We call this graph,
S_{n,k}
.
Any graph with at most
n
vertices and maximum degree at most
k
has an immersion into
S_{n,k}

[
Wollan15
]
.

The wall graph
H_{n}
is obtained from the
n\times n
grid graph by removing in each even row every even vertical edge and in each odd row, each odd edge vertical edge.
An illustration is given by Wollan
[
Wollan15
]
and they prove that for all
k\in\N^{+}
, we have
k\leq{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(H_{2k^{2}}\right)}{\tcwOp(H_{2k^{2}})}{\tcwOp(H_{2k^{2}})}{\tcwOp(H_{2k^{2}})}}
.
In particular,
H_{n}
has
2n^{2}
vertices and maximum degree 3.

Kim et al.
[
KimOPST18
]
provide an algorithm, that given a number
w\in\N
either provides a tree-cut decomposition of width at most
2w
, or certifies that
{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(G\right)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}{\tcwOp(G)}}>w
.
Its running time is
2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(w^{2}\log 2\right)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log 2)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log 2)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log 2)}}{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}^{2}
.

Slim Tree-Cut Width

The
slim tree-cut width
is defined very similarly to tree-cut width.
Consider a tree-cut decomposition
\mathcal{D}=(T,\mathcal{X})
and a node
t\in V(T)
.
Define the 2-center
\tilde{H}^{2;\mathcal{D}}_{t}
of
t
in
\mathcal{D}
to be the graph obtained from
H^{\mathcal{D}}_{t}
after exhaustively suppressing all vertices of degree at most
1
.

Definition 4
.

Let
\alpha
be the adhesion of
\mathcal{D}
.
The
slim width
of
\mathcal{D}
is defined to be

\max\{\alpha\}\cup\left\{{\mathchoice{\left|\tilde{V}(H^{2,\mathcal{D}}_{t})\right|}{{|\tilde{V}(H^{2,\mathcal{D}}_{t})|}}{{|\tilde{V}(H^{2,\mathcal{D}}_{t})|}}{{|\tilde{V}(H^{2,\mathcal{D}}_{t})|}}}\right\}_{t\in V(T)}.

The slim tree-cut width
{\mathchoice{\stcwOp\left(G\right)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}}
is the smallest slim tree-cut width of any tree-cut width for
G
.
Ganian et al.
[
GanianK22
]
provide an algorithm, that given a number
w\in\N
either provides a nice tree-cut decomposition of slim width at most
6(w+1)^{3}
, or certifies that
{\mathchoice{\stcwOp\left(G\right)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}}>w
.
Its running time is
2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(w^{2}\log w\right)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log w)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log w)}{\OOp(w^{2}\log w)}}{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}^{4}
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be such that
G^{\prime}
has an immersion into
G
, then
{\mathchoice{\stcwOp\left(G^{\prime}\right)}{\stcwOp(G^{\prime})}{\stcwOp(G^{\prime})}{\stcwOp(G^{\prime})}}\leq{\mathchoice{\stcwOp\left(G\right)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}{\stcwOp(G)}}

[
GanianK22
]
.
Let
n\in\N^{+}
and consider the graph of
n
cycles of length three that all contain the same vertex
c
, but are otherwise vertex disjoint.
We call this graph
W_{n}
and the collection of all such graphs windmills.
For all
r\in\N\setminus{\mathchoice{\left[1\right]_{0}}{[1]_{0}}{[1]_{0}}{[1]_{0}}}
, we have
{\mathchoice{\stcwOp\left(W_{r^{2}}\right)}{\stcwOp(W_{r^{2}})}{\stcwOp(W_{r^{2}})}{\stcwOp(W_{r^{2}})}}\geq r
, while
{\mathchoice{\tcwOp\left(W_{r}\right)}{\tcwOp(W_{r})}{\tcwOp(W_{r})}{\tcwOp(W_{r})}}=2
.
An illustration of windmill graphs is given by Ganian et al.
[
GanianK22
]
.

Feedback Edge Set

Let
S\subseteq E(G)
.
If
G-E
is cyclefree, we call
S
a
feedback edge set
.
The size of the smallest feedback edge set is called the
feedback edge set number

{\mathchoice{\fenOp\left(G\right)}{\fenOp(G)}{\fenOp(G)}{\fenOp(G)}}
of
G
.
Note that
{\mathchoice{\fenOp\left(G\right)}{\fenOp(G)}{\fenOp(G)}{\fenOp(G)}}={\mathchoice{\left|E(G)\right|}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}{{|E(G)|}}}-{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G\right)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}}\right|}{{|{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G\right)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G\right)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G\right)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}{\compOp(G)}}|}}}
.

Treewidth + Maximum Degree

This parameter is just
{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}+\max_{v\in V(G)}{\mathchoice{\degOp\left(v\right)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}}
and gives a simple approach to turning
{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G\right)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}{\twOp(G)}}
into a parameter which ensures that there are small edge cuts that if removed disconnect the graph into substantially smaller parts.

3
Augmentation for
\gstp

For the structural parameterization of
\edp
the tool of the augmented graph has found a wide range of applications.
For this consider an instance
(G,P)
of
\edp
, where
G=(V,E)
represents the underlying graph and
P\subseteq\binom{V}{2}
the pairs of vertices that ought to be connected.
The augmented graph of this instance is denoted by
{G}^{P}
and is defined to be
G+P
, the graph obtained from
G
where we additionally connect all pairs of
P
.
This concept was first introduced for
Multicut
by Gottlob and Lie
[
GottlobL07
]
.

For
\edp
, structural parameterization by a parameter derived from the augmented graph, opens many doors.
As an example, consider the vertex cover number as a parameter.
For graphs with vertex cover number at least
3
this problem is
NP
-hard
[
Fleszar2018Sep
]
.
If we restrict our view to graph with vertex cover number at most
2
, this problem is solvable in polynomial time
[
GanianOR21
]
.
This strict dichotomy is not necessarily desired and it does not help to fully understand which instances are actually hard.
However, taking the vertex cover number of the augmented graph as the parameter,
\edp
is fixed-parameter tractable.
This even holds for the weaker parameter of the fracture number of the augmented graph
[
GanianOR21
]
.

We want to transfer this approach to
\gstp
.
It is not directly clear how this should be done.
Consider an instance
(G,\mathcal{T},d)
of
\gstp
.
There are at least two viable options on how to approach this.
First, for each
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
and distinct
u,v\in T_{i}
we could add an edge between
u
and
v
, creating parallel edges if this edge exist already.
As this ensures that all
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
form a clique, we call this the clique-augmented graph
G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}
.
There is a simple reduction for
\edp
that ensures that all terminals are non-adjacent and have degree one
[
Fleszar2018Sep
]
.
Assuming, each
v\in V
is part of at most
{\mathchoice{\degOp\left(v\right)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}}
terminal pairs, which can be ensured by applying
, all considered parameters, but vertex-cover number, only grow slightly as a function of the parameter.
Namely, fracture number grows at most quadratically, while all other considered parameters grow by at most a factor of two.
Therefore, the clique-augmented graph matches the augmented graph for
\edp
in the cases considered in previous research.

On the other hand, we can introduce a new vertex
T_i for each
T_{i}\in\mathcal{T}
and connect it to all
v\in T_{i}
.
We call this the vertex-augmented graph
G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}
.
This version differs from the augmented graph for
\edp
by subdividing the inserted edges.
We now study, how parameterized complexity results transfer between the two versions with respect to the structural parameters presented in
Figures

1
and
2
.
To this end, we check for each such parameter
\kappa
, whether or not there is a function
f\colon\N\to\N
such that for all instances
(G,\mathcal{T},d)
of
\gstp
we have
\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})\leq f(\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}))
and vice versa.
In this case, we write
\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})\leq_{f}\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})
.
Note that
d
does not play any role in this definition.
If such a function exists, any result showing that
\gstp
—or
\edp
, or
\stp
—is
\fpt
with respect to
\kappa
of the vertex-augmented graph, yields directly that
\gstp
is
\fpt
with respect to
\kappa
of the clique augmented graph.
Similarly, if
\gstp
is
\wonehard
with respect to
\kappa
of the clique-augmented graph, it is
\wonehard
with respect to
\kappa
of the vertex-augmented graph as well.
That is, algorithmic results would be stronger with respect to the vertex-augmented graph and hardness results would be stronger with respect to the clique augmented graph.
We now show, that for
\kappa\in\{\twOp,\fvsOp,\fracNumOp,\twOp+\maxDegOp,\tcwOp,\stcwOp,\fenOp\}
such a function in deed exists. For
\kappa=\twOp+\maxDegOp
, the reverse holds as well.

Lemma 5
.

For all
\kappa\in\{\twOp,\fvsOp,\fracNumOp,\tcwOp,\stcwOp,\fenOp,\twOp+\maxDegOp\}
,
\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})\leq_{f}\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})
.
If
\kappa=\twOp+\maxDegOp
, we additionally have
\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})\leq_{f}\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})
.

Proof.

\bm{\twOp\!.}
Let
(S,\mathcal{X})
be a tree decomposition of
G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}
.
We create a tree decomposition based on
(S,\mathcal{X})
for
G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}
, by choosing for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
a bag
X_{s}\in\mathcal{X}
with
T\subseteq X_{s}
, which exists as
T
is a clique in
G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}

[
BodlaenderM90
]
.
We add a node
s^{\prime}
associated with the bag
{{T\cup\{\aug{T}\lx@text@rbrace$ as a child of $s$.
Note that this is a tree decomposition for $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ with width at most one larger than the treewidth of $(S,\mathcal{X})$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\fvsOp\!.}$\quad Let $S$ be a feedback vertex set for $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
We claim that $S$ is also a feedback vertex set for $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.
Assume there is a cycle $C$ in $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S$ and set $C^{\prime}$ to be $C$ with all vertices in $\aug{\mathcal{T}}$ removed.
As for every $T\in\mathcal{T}$ the vertices in $T$ are adjacent in $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$, $C^{\prime}$ is a cycle in $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S$ as well, violating the fact that $S$ is a feedback vertex set.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\fracNumOp\!.}$\quad Let $S$ be a fracture modulator of $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
We now add arbitrary vertices to $S$ until it is a fracture modulator for $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.
Note that the obtained set is a fracture modulator and its size is bounded by ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+\max_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S\right)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}}}{\mathchoice{\left|V(C)\right|}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}}$.
Let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S\right)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}}$.
All that remains is to bound ${\mathchoice{\left|V(C)\right|}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}}$ in terms of $S$.
As no pair of augmented vertices is adjacent, if $C$ contains only augmented vertices, we have ${\mathchoice{\left|C\right|}{{|C|}}{{|C|}}{{|C|}}}=1$.
Otherwise, there is a vertex $v\in V(C)$ that is not augmented.
Let $D\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S\right)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}{\compOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S)}}$ be such that $v\in V(D)$.
We now show, that $V(C)\cap V(G)\subseteq V(D)$.
Let $u\in V(C)\cap V(G)$.
There is a $vu$-path $P$ in $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S$.
Note that $P$ with all augmented vertices removed is a path in $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}-S$.
Thus, $u\in V(D)$.
\par Now, consider any $T\in\mathcal{T}$ with $\aug{T} \in V(C)$.
As there is a path from $v$ to $\aug{T}$ in $C$, we have $T\cap V(C)\neq\emptyset$ and, in particular, $T\subseteq S\cup V(C)$.
Thus, there are at most $2^{{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|V(C)\right|}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}}}\leq 2^{2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}$ many choices for $T$ and at most that many augmented vertices contained in $V(C)$; yielding that the obtained fracture modulator has size at most $2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+2^{2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\tcwOp\!.}$\quad Let $(S,\mathcal{X})$ be a tree-cut decomposition of $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ and let its tree cut width be $w$.
To obtain a tree-cut decomposition for $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$, consider any $T\in\mathcal{T}$ and let $s_{T}\in V(S)$ be a node of $S$ containing a vertex of $T$ such that no vertex of $T$ is contained in a descendant of $s_{T}$ in $S$.
We add the vertex $\aug{T}$ to the bag associated with the node $s_{T}$.
Doing this for all $T$ does not change the adhesion of any node.
So, we need to bound the increase in torso size.
In the torso at $s_{T}$, the vertex $\aug{T}$ is only connected to vertices in $X_{s_{T}}$ and the vertex $z_{\mathrm{top}}$ to which $V(G)\setminus Y_{s_{T}}$ was contracted.
However, the degrees of all vertices in the torso at $s_{T}$ with respect to $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ are bounded by their degrees with respect to $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ and the graph induced by all pendant vertices did not change.
This means, that any contraction sequence of pendant vertices with respect to $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ is also viable with respect to $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.
So, we only need to bound the number of new core vertices in $s_{T}$, this means, bound the number of $T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}$ with $s_{T}=s_{T^{\prime}}$.
All such $T^{\prime}$ have $X_{s_{T}}\cap T^{\prime}\neq\emptyset$.
There are at most $2^{{\mathchoice{\left|X_{s_{T}}\right|}{{|X_{s_{T}}|}}{{|X_{s_{T}}|}}{{|X_{s_{T}}|}}}}$ such $T^{\prime}$ with $T^{\prime}\subseteq X_{s_{T}}$. So, assume $T^{\prime}\not\subseteq X_{s_{T}}$.
As all descendants of $s_{T}$ in $S$ are disjoint from $T^{\prime}$, we have $T^{\prime}\setminus Y_{s_{T}}\neq\emptyset$, meaning that $T^{\prime}$ contributes at least one edge to the adhesion of $s_{T}$.
Thus, there are at most $w$ with $T^{\prime}\not\subseteq X_{s_{T}}$ and $w+2^{w}$ overall.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\stcwOp\!.}$\quad The proof is analogous to the one for tree-cut width.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\fenOp\!.}$\quad Let $S\subseteq E(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})$ be a feedback edge set of $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.
To obtain a feedback edge set for $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ let $\mathcal{T}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{T}$ be the terminal sets which added an edge to $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ that is contained in $S$.
Now, consider the edge set $S^{\prime}$ that contains $S\cap E(G)$ and for each $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ all but one edge of $\{\aug{T}t\lx@text@rbrace_{t \in T}$.
We notice that $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}-S^{\prime}$ is a subgraph of $G-S$ with some additional one degree vertices. Therefore, $S^{\prime}$ is a feedback edge set of $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
\par Now, we show that $|S^{\prime}|\leq 2|S|$.
As all the edges contributed by each $T\in\mathcal{T}$ are distinct, it is sufficient to show that each $T\in\mathcal{T}$ contributes at most twice as many edge to $S^{\prime}$ compared to $S$.
If $|T|\leq 2$, the amount of contributed edges is equal.
So, let $|T|\geq 3$.
As the edges contributed by $T$ to $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ form a clique, at least $\binom{|T|}{2}-({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-1)=\frac{({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-1)({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-2)}{2}$ of them are contained in $S$.
On the other hand, at most ${\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-1$ are contributed to $S^{\prime}$ by $T$.
Therefore, $T$ contributes at most $\frac{2({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-1)}{({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-1)({\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}-2)}\leq 2$ times as many edges to $S^{\prime}$ as it does to $S$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@ $\bm{\twOp+\maxDegOp\!.}$\quad We know that ${\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}\leq 1+{\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}}$.
So, we only need to bound ${\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}$. To bound ${\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}$ in terms of ${\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}}$, let $v\in V(G)$ and set $\mathcal{T}_{v}\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{T}\mid v\in T\}$ to be the terminal sets containing $v$.
Then, $v$ is adjacent in $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ to all its neighbors in $G$ and all vertices for which there is a terminal set containing both, that is ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}={\mathchoice{\left|N_{G}(v)\cup((\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v})\setminus\{v\})\right|}{{|N_{G}(v)\cup((\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v})\setminus\{v\})|}}{{|N_{G}(v)\cup((\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v})\setminus\{v\})|}}{{|N_{G}(v)\cup((\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v})\setminus\{v\})|}}}\geq\max({\mathchoice{\degOp_{G}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}},{\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}-1))$.
Additionally, in $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ the vertex $v$ is adjacent to all its neighbors in $G$ and the augmented vertices $\aug{\mathcal{T}_s}$, that is ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}={\mathchoice{\degOp_{G}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}}+{\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}$.
For all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{s}$, we have $\{v\}\subseteq T\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}$.
Thus, ${\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\leq 2^{{\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}-1}$ and we get ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}\leq{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}+2^{{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}}$.
Now, consider an augmented vertex $u\in V(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})$.
Choose, $v\in{\mathchoice{\augOp^{-1}\left(u\right)}{\augOp^{-1}(u)}{\augOp^{-1}(u)}{\augOp^{-1}(u)}}$.
Then, ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}={\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}+1$.
So, the parameter $\twOp+\maxDegOp$ of $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ is bounded by a function of its value on $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
\par Now, let $(S,\mathcal{X})$ be a tree decomposition of $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.
To create a tree decomposition for $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$, replace for each $T\in\mathcal{T}$ every occurrence of $\aug{T}$ in $\mathcal{X}$ with $T$.
Notice that this is a tree decomposition.
Consider $T\in\mathcal{T}$.
We have that ${\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}={\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(\aug{T}\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(\aug{T})}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(\aug{T})}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(\aug{T})}} \leq{\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}$.
Therefore, the treewidth of $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ is bounded by $({\mathchoice{\twOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\twOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}+1)({\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}+1)$.
Consider any $v\in V(G)$.
We have ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}\leq{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}{\degOp_{G}(v)}}+{\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}$.
Observe that ${\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{v}}{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}$ and that ${\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}{\degOp_{G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}}(v)}}$ and that $\max_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{v}}{\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}$.
Thus, ${\mathchoice{\left|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}\right|}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}{{|\bigcup\mathcal{T}_{v}|}}}\leq({\mathchoice{\maxDegOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\maxDegOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}})^{2}$, yielding that the maximum degree of $G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ is also bounded by a function of the parameter with respect to $G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$.\qed\end{itemize}
\end@proof
\par Next, we show that for the only other considered parameter, namely vertex cover number, such a function bounding ${\mathchoice{\vcOp\left(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})}}$ in terms of ${\mathchoice{\vcOp\left(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}\right)}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}{\vcOp(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})}}$ can not exist.
\par\begin{lemma}There exists a family of instances $(G_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i},d_{i})_{i\in\N}$ of $\gstp$ such that $\{{\mathchoice{\vcOp\left(G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T}_{i})}\right)}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}}\}_{i\in\N}$ is bounded and $\{{\mathchoice{\vcOp\left(G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})}\right)}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}{\vcOp(G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})}}\}_{i\in\N}$ is unbounded.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $i\in\N$ and consider the star graph $S_{i}$ with $i$ leaves and center vertex $c$.
For each $v\in V(S_{i})\setminus\{c\}$, set $T_{v}\coloneqq\{c,v\}$ to be a terminal set and let $\mathcal{T}_{i}\coloneqq\{T_{v}\}_{v\in V(S_{i})\setminus\{c\}}$.
As all edges that clique-augmentation adds are present in $S_{i}$ and vertex covers are not affected by parallel edges, any vertex cover of $S_{i}$ is a vertex cover of $S_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
In particular, $\{c\}$ is a vertex cover of $S_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
On the other hand $S_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})}$ is the windmill $W_{i}$, which has a matching of size $i$.
Thus, its vertex cover number is $i$.
\qed\end@proof
\par Finally, we show that for all considered $\kappa$, except for $\twOp+\maxDegOp$, there also is no function bounding $\kappa$ of the clique-augmented graph in terms of $\kappa$ with respect to the vertex augmented graph.
\par\begin{lemma}For all $\kappa\in\{\twOp,\fvsOp,\fracNumOp,\vcOp,\tcwOp,\stcwOp,\fenOp\}$, there exists a family of instances $(G_{i},\mathcal{T}_{i},d_{i})_{i\in\N}$ of $\gstp$ such that $\{\kappa(G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})\}_{i\in\N}$ is bounded and $\{\kappa(G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T}_{i})})\}_{i\in\N}$ is unbounded.
\end{lemma}
\@proof First, we prove this result for $\kappa\in\{\twOp,\fvsOp,\fracNumOp,\vcOp\}$.
For this let $i\in\N$ and consider the star graph $S_{i}$ with $i$ leaves around the center vertex $c$.
Set the single terminal set $T$ to be equal to all leaves.
The graph $S_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ has a clique with $i$ vertices as a subgraph; so, its treewidth is at least $i-1$.
Whereas in the graph $S_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ the set $\{c,\aug{T}\lx@text@rbrace$ is a vertex cover of size 2.
We have that $\kappa(S_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})$ is bounded by a function of the vertex cover number, yielding the result.
\par Now, let $\kappa\in\{\tcwOp,\stcwOp\}$ and $i\in\N$.
Consider the graph $G_{i}$ which consists of a vertex $c$ and $4i^{4}$ isolated paths of length $3$.
Denote with $\{P_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathchoice{\left[4i^{4}\right]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}}}$ these isolated paths and set $\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\{\{c\}\cup V(P_{j})\}_{j\in{\mathchoice{\left[4i^{4}\right]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}}}$.
See $G_{i}$, $G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$, and $G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ illustrated in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{fig:tcw-augmentation}.
Notice that the graph $S_{4i^{4},3}$, which is the star graph on $i^{2}$ vertices where each leave has $3$ parallel edges to the center, immerses into $G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$.
As the wall $H_{2i^{2}}$ has an immersion into $S_{4i^{4},3}$~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Wollan15}{}{}]}, it also has an immersion into $G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$; so, $G_{i}^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ has tree-cut width at least $i$~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Wollan15}{}{}]}.
For $G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$, we obtain a tree-cut decomposition as follows.
We set the root to be a bag containing only $c$.
For each $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[4i^{4}\right]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}{[4i^{4}]}}$, we create a bag as a child of the root node containing $\{\aug{\lx@text@lbrace c\lx@text@rbrace \cup V(P_j)} \lx@text@rbrace\cup V(P_j)$.
This tree-cut decomposition is drawn in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{fig:tcw-augmentation-vertex}.
We see that this tree-cut decomposition has width and slim width at most $4$.
\par\begin{figure}\centering\subfigure[t]{0.32}

\subfigure[t]{0.32}
\centering\includegraphics[width=310.4979pt]{tcw-augmentation-vertex.pdf}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{4}}{ The graph $G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$\!.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 4}}{ The graph $G_{i}^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$\!.}}}\@add@centering

We draw augmented vertices and edges in orange and the tree-cut decomposition in blue.}}}\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par Finally, for $\kappa=\fenOp$ and $i\in\N$, we consider the graph $G^{\prime}_{i}$ on $3i$ isolated vertices.
Partition $V(G_{i})$ into sets $T_{1},T_{2},\dots,T_{i}$ of three vertices each and set $\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\{T_{j}\}_{j\in{\mathchoice{\left[i\right]}{[i]}{[i]}{[i]}}}$.
We notice that $G_{i}^{\prime\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})}$ is a union of $i$ triangle graphs.
Thus, it has feedback edge number $i$.
Additionally, we see that $G_{i}^{\prime\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})}$ is a forest.
Consequently, it has feedback edge number $0$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{table}\centering\begin{tabular}[]{lcc}\hline\cr\hline\cr Parameter&$\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})\leq_{f}\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})$&$\kappa(G^{\mathrm{cliq}(\mathcal{T})})\leq_{f}\kappa(G^{\mathrm{vert}(\mathcal{T})})$\\
\hline\cr$\twOp$&\checkmark&$\times$\\
$\fvsOp$&\checkmark&$\times$\\
$\fracNumOp$&\checkmark&$\times$\\

$\twOp+\maxDegOp$&\checkmark&\checkmark\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{ Summary of the results obtained in \lx@cref{creftypepluralcap~refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-bounded}, \lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-unbounded} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:clique-augment-unbounded}. For each parameter and statement, we write \checkmark if the statement holds with respect to this parameter and $\times$ if it does not.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 2}}{ Summary of the results obtained in \lx@cref{creftypepluralcap~refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-bounded}, \lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-unbounded} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:clique-augment-unbounded}. For each parameter and statement, we write \checkmark if the statement holds with respect to this parameter and $\times$ if it does not.}}}\@add@centering\end{table}
\par The results obtained by \lx@cref{creftypepluralcap~refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-bounded}, \lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:vert-augment-unbounded} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{stmt:clique-augment-unbounded} are summarized in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{tbl:summary-augmentation}.
We see that the structural parameter of the vertex-augmented graph is strictly stronger with respect almost all considered parameters.
Only for vertex cover number they are incomparable and functionally equivalent for sum of tree width and maximum degree.
In light of these results and noticing that we mainly provide $\fpt$-algorithms, we decide to focus our attention on the vertex-augmented graph.
From now on, we simply call the vertex-augmented graph the augmented graph for $\gstp$ and denote it with ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
\par
\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{\texorpdfstring{$\gstp$}{GSTP} is \texorpdfstring{$\fpt$}{FPT} by the Augmented Fracture Number}
In this Chapter we show that $\gstp$ is fixed-parameter tractable by the fracture number of the augmented graph.
This is an important stepping stone towards more intricate results, like showing that this problem is fixed-parameter tractable by the tree-cut width of the augmented graph.
Which in turn we can make use of to show that $\stp$ is fixed-parameter tractable by the tree-cut width of the input graph.
\par To achieve the result of this Chapter, we represent the instance as an integer linear program with the number of variables bounded by a computable function of the parameter (i.e., the fracture number of the augmented graph).
Since checking whether an interger linear program has a feasible solution is $\fpt$ in the number of variabel~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Kannan87}{}{}]}, this yields an $\fpt$-algorithm, given that we can find a fracture modulator in $\fpt$-time as well.
This was claimed by Dvorák et~al.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{DvorakEGKO21}{}{}]}, but their proof suffers from a minor inaccuracy.
We examine this and a corrected version of their algorithm in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{sec:find-frac-mod-in-fpt}.
\par Our approach to represent the instance as an integer linear program uses similar ideas as Ganian et~al.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GanianOR21}{}{}]} use to show that $\edp$ is $\fpt$ by the fracture number of the augmented graph.
We first consider all the ways a component of the graph without the fracture modulator can interact with the remaining components.
Then, we group the components and we, finally, provide an integer linear program to represent the whole instance.
In doing so, we not only generalize there algorithm significantly, but we also reduce the running time from triply exponential in the parameter to doubly exponential in the parameter.
The exponential-time-hypothesis implies that there is no algorithm solving $\ilp$ with $n$ variables in $2^{o(n)}$ time for all such instances.
Assuming $\ethOp$, to decrease the running time to sub-doubly-exponential, either we need to use less than exponentially many variables to represent the instance, utilize some structure in the obtained $\ilp$ instances, or switch to a new approach avoiding integer linear programming altogether.
All of which seem quite challenging.
\par Before we start into the three main parts of the proof, we provide common definitions and reduction rules.
Let $(G,\mathcal{T},d)$ be an instance of $\gstp$, let $X\subseteq V({G}^{\mathcal{T}})$ be a fracture modulator of ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
For all $U\subseteq V({G}^{\mathcal{T}})$, we call the set of all $T\in\mathcal{T}$ with $\aug{T}\in U$ by $\mathcal{T}_{U}$.
Let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}}$ and denote with $C^{+}\coloneqq{G}^{\mathcal{T}}[V(C)\cup X]$ the subgraph induced on the component and the fracture modulator.
\par For this Chapter, we need the following reduction rules.
They allow us to show, that in all relevant instances, most terminal sets only require few subgraphs to be assigned to them.
\par\begin{reductionrule}If there is a $T\in\mathcal{T}$ with $|T|<2$, remove $T$ from $\mathcal{T}$.
\end{reductionrule}
\@proof As we just remove constraints, whenever the original instance is a positive instance, so is the reduced instance.
Now assume that the reduced instance is a positive instance and let $\mathcal{X}$ be a solution.
The set $T$ can be interpreted as either an empty or a single-vertex tree.
In either case, it does not contain any edges and connects all vertices in $T$.
Thus, we can add $T$ to $\mathcal{X}$ $d(T)$-times and obtain a solution for the original instance.
\qed\end@proof
\begin{reductionrule}After applying \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:sensible-terminal-sets} exhaustively, if there is a $v\in V$ such that $\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}\colon v\in T}d(T)>{\mathchoice{\degOp\left(v\right)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}}$, output a trivial negative instance.
\end{reductionrule}
\@proof We show that no positive instance satisfies the premise.
Consider any solution, let $v\in V$ and denote with $\mathcal{F}_{v}$ the trees in the solution that contain $v$.
Since for all $T\in\mathcal{T}$ we have $|T|\geq 2$, any tree in the solution contains an edge adjacent to every one of its vertices.
As all different trees are edge-disjoint, $|\mathcal{F}_{v}|\leq{\mathchoice{\degOp\left(v\right)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}}$ and by definition of $\mathcal{F}_{v}$ we have $\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}\colon v\in T}d(T)\leq|\mathcal{F}_{v}|$.
\qed\end@proof
\par To better assess, which terminal sets might need special attention, and to simplify our proofs, we only consider fracture modulators with a particular structure.
\par\begin{definition}Let $X\subseteq V({G}^{\mathcal{T}})$ be a fracture modulator of ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
We call $X$ \emph{nice}, if
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$G[X]$ is edgeless,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{X}$, we have that there are two distinct components $C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ with $V(C)\cap T\neq\emptyset$ and $V(C^{\prime})\cap T\neq\emptyset$.\end{enumerate}\end{definition}
\par This is not a strong requirement.
In fact, we can always turn a fracture modulator into a nice fracture modulator of similar size.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $X$ be a fracture modulator of ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
There is an equivalent instance $\mathscr{P}^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},\mathcal{T},d)$ and a nice fracture modulator $S$ of ${G^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T}}$ with ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}$ and ${\mathchoice{\left|V(G^{\prime})\right|}{{|V(G^{\prime})|}}{{|V(G^{\prime})|}}{{|V(G^{\prime})|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|V(G)\right|}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}{{|V(G)|}}}+\binom{{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}}{2}+2{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}$.
We can construct $\mathscr{P}^{\prime}$ and $S$ in linear time.
\end{lemma}
\@proof First, we subdivide each edge of $G[X]$, which adds at most $\binom{{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}}{2}$ vertices.
Call this graph $H$.
