Let $Y\coloneqq\{\aug{T} \mid T \in\mathcal{T}_X; \exists C \in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}} - S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}} - S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}} - S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}} - S)}}\colon T \subseteq V(C^+)\lx@text@rbrace$ be the vertices of $X$, that violate the condition of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:nice-fracture-modulator-TS} and set $Z\coloneqq X\setminus Y$.
The graph ${H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z$ can have connected components of size larger than $Z$.
In order to obtain a fracture modulator, we new isolated vertices to $H$ and $Z$, obtaining $G^{\prime}$ and $S$, until $S$ is fracture modulator of ${G^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
We notice that $S$ is a nice.
It remains to bound the size of $S$.
\par Consider a $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z\right)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z)}}$ with ${\mathchoice{\left|C\right|}{{|C|}}{{|C|}}{{|C|}}}>2$.
As the vertices created by subdividing the edges of $G[X]$ combined with $Y$ form an independent set in ${H}^{\mathcal{T}}$, there is a $D\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}}$ with $V(C)\cap V(D)\neq\emptyset$ and, in particular, $V(C)\supseteq V(D)$.
Let $Y^{*}\coloneqq\{y\in Y\mid N(y)\cap V(D)\neq\emptyset\}$.
By definition of $Y$, we have for all $y\in Y^{*}$ that $N_{{G}^{\mathcal{T}}}[y]\subseteq V(D)\cup Z\cup\{y\}$.
Additionally, for all $d\in V(D)$, we have $N_{{G}^{\mathcal{T}}}[d]\subseteq V(D)\cup Z\cup Y^{*}$.
So, $N_{{G}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z}[V(D)\cup Y^{*}]=V(D)\cup Y^{*}$.
As $V(C)$ contains all $V(D)\cup Y^{*}$, there are no other vertices in $V(C)$ since the vertices in $V(D)\cup Y^{*}$ would be disconnected from them in ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z$ as well as ${H}^{\mathcal{T}}-Z$.
Thus, $V(C)=V(D)\cup Y^{*}$ and ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}=\max(1,{\mathchoice{\left|Z\right|}{{|Z|}}{{|Z|}}{{|Z|}}},\max\{{\mathchoice{\left|V(C)\right|}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}{{|V(C)|}}}\}_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({H}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}})\leq{\mathchoice{\left|Z\right|}{{|Z|}}{{|Z|}}{{|Z|}}}+\max_{D\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-X)}}}{\mathchoice{\left|V(D)\right|}{{|V(D)|}}{{|V(D)|}}{{|V(D)|}}}+{\mathchoice{\left|Y\right|}{{|Y|}}{{|Y|}}{{|Y|}}}\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}$, meaning that we add at most $2{\mathchoice{\left|X\right|}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}{{|X|}}}$ isolated vertices to $H$.
\qed\end@proof
\par From now on, we assume that $S$ is a nice fracture modulator for $(G,\mathcal{T},d)$ and that \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance} has been applied.
Note that even though $G[S]$ is edgeless ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}[S]$ may contain edges.
Denote with $\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ the set of all $T\in\mathcal{T}$ satisfying $T\subseteq S$.
We show, that in all relevant instances and for all $T\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}^{\star}$, the number of required subsets $d(T)$ is bounded by $2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\par\begin{lemma}After applying \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance}, for all $T\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ we have $d(T)\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Consider a $T\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}^{\star}$.
Then, there is a $v\in T\setminus S$ and let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ be such that $v\in V(C)$.
Since $N(v)\subseteq S\cup C$, we have ${\mathchoice{\degOp\left(v\right)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}{\degOp(v)}}\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$ and since we applied \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance}, this shows that $d(T)\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Component Configurations}
To fulfill the connectivity requirements, each component might need to use edges from other components, or other components might need to use edges from this component.
We now characterize how a component $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ can interact with the remaining instance.
To this end, we introduce the concept of a \emph{component-configuration}.
The goal of this Section is to categorize positive and negative instances solely based on the configurations of the components.
\par As ${\mathchoice{\left|V(C^{+})\right|}{{|V(C^{+})|}}{{|V(C^{+})|}}{{|V(C^{+})|}}}\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$, denote with $u\coloneqq\binom{2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}{2}$ the maximum number of edges in edges in $C^{+}$ and define a configuration of $C$ as a tuple $(\demandOp,\supplyOp,\assignOp)$ with $\demandOp,\supplyOp\colon 2^{S\cap V(G)}\to{\mathchoice{\left[u\right]_{0}}{[u]_{0}}{[u]_{0}}{[u]_{0}}}$ and $\assignOp\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\times{\mathchoice{\left[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\times{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\to 2^{S\cap V(G)}$.
For convenience, let $\gamma$ be a component-configuration, then we may write $\demandOp_{\gamma}$, $\supplyOp_{\gamma}$, and $\assignOp_{\gamma}$ for its first, second, and third component respectively.
\par The first part (i.e., $\demandOp$) signifies how often each subset of the fracture modulator gets connected by other components and used for the connection requirements of $\mathcal{T}_{C}$~--~what is the additional demand for each subset?
The second component (i.e., $\supplyOp$) signifies how often each subset of the fracture modulator gets connected inside this component, but these connections are \emph{not} used to satisfy connection requirements of terminal sets in $\mathcal{T}_{C}$~--~what is the additional supply for each subset?
\par Finally, consider a terminal set $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$.
We have $T\notin\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and we have applied \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance}; so, by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{stmt:terminal-sets-few-requirements} we have $d(T)\leq 2|S|$.
This allows us to explicitly store the contribution of $C$ to every tree assigned to such a terminal set which is necessary since $T$ can span an arbitrary number of components in ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S$.
For each $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, the information required for the $i$-th tree assigned to $T$ is stored in ${\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,\cdot\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,\cdot)}{\assignOp(T,i,\cdot)}{\assignOp(T,i,\cdot)}}$.
Call this tree $F^{i}$.
Let $\sigma\colon{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\to V(C)$ be a surjection.
Then, we can imagine for all $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$ that ${\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}$ is a set of vertices in $S$ which $\sigma(j)$ can reach via $F^{i}[V(C^{+})]$.
Note that it might not be all such vertices.
So, we can think of the third argument like an index of a vertex in $C$.
We do not use the actual vertices here to be able to easily say that two components are in the same configuration.
\par Not every component can be in any configuration in a valid solution.
For example, a component with $k$ edges can not supply more than $k$ additional connections to the other components.
To capture this concept, we say a component \emph{admits} a configuration if it can locally satisfy all the requirements of the configuration and of the terminal sets in $\mathcal{T}_{C}$.
\par To make this rigorous, let $\gamma=(\demandOp,\supplyOp,\assignOp)$ be a component-configuration.
We now provide an instances of $\gstp$ to help us define when $C$ admits a configuration $\gamma$.
To do so, choose $\sigma\colon{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\to V(C)$ to be a surjection, which we use to encode $\assignOp$ as explained above.
This instance is called ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$.
\par First, we define the host-graph of the instance ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$.
For this, we start with the graph $C^{+}$ and for each $Q\subseteq S\cap V(G)$, we add ${\mathchoice{\demandOp\left(Q\right)}{\demandOp(Q)}{\demandOp(Q)}{\demandOp(Q)}}$-many vertices $v$ to the graph with $N(v)=Q$.
Denote this graph with $H$.
\par Now, we define the terminal sets, that need to be connected.
Denote for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$ and $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$ the set $A(T,i,U,\sigma)\coloneqq\{\sigma(j)\mid j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}};{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}=U\}$ the set of vertices in $C$ that is assigned to $U$ to satisfy connections for the $i$-th tree of $T$.
Now let ${\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\emptyset\neq U\subseteq S\cap V(G)\colon A(T,i,U,\sigma)\neq\emptyset}\{U\cup A(T,i,U,\sigma)\}$ be the subsets of $S\cap V(G)$ that get assigned some vertex unioned with the assigned vertices.
Let
\@@amsalign
Note that if $C$ consists of exactly one augmented vertex, $\mathcal{Q}$ is empty; otherwise $\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{T}_{C}$.
Further, the set $\mathcal{A}$ might intersect $Q$, but one can verify that $\mathcal{S}$ is disjoint from $\mathcal{A}$ and $Q$.
The complete set of terminal sets, for which we need connections, is $\mathcal{U}\coloneqq Q\cup\mathcal{S}\cup\mathcal{A}$.
\par Finally, we need to specify the required number of connections $d^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\N^{+}$.
For this extend $d,\supplyOp$ and $\assignOp$ canonically to yield 0 or $\emptyset$ on arguments not in their original domain.
For all $U\in\mathcal{U}$ let $$d^{\prime}(U)\coloneqq d(U)+{\mathchoice{\supplyOp\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}}+\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}}{\mathchoice{\left|\left\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\right\}\right|}{{|\left\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\right\}|}}{{|\left\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\right\}|}}{{|\left\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\right\}|}}},$$ and define ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}=(H,\mathcal{U},d^{\prime})$.
\par\begin{definition}We say a component $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ admits a configuration $\gamma$, if there is a surjection $\sigma\colon{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\to V(C)$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@for all $T\in\mathcal{T_{S}}$, $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, and $j\in\sigma^{-1}(T\cap V(C))$, we have ${\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}\neq\emptyset$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@there is a solution $(\mathcal{F},\pi)$ to ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@for all $F\in\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{S})$, we have that $V(F)\subseteq V(C^{+})$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $v\in V(H)\setminus V(C^{+})$ where $H$ is the host-graph of ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$, there is exactly one $F\in\mathcal{F}$ with $v\in V(F)$ and for this $F$, we have ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{F}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{F}(v)}{\degOp_{F}(v)}{\degOp_{F}(v)}}\geq 2$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $F\in\mathcal{F}$, we have $E(C^{+})\cap E(F)\neq\emptyset$ and $F$ is cycle-free.\end{enumerate}\end{enumerate}\end{definition}
\par We say that $(\sigma,\mathcal{F},\pi)$ \emph{gives rise to} $\gamma$ \emph{on} $C$.
With \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:admit-terminal-set-assigned} we ensure for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ that every $v\in T\cap V(C)$ is connected to a vertex in $S\cap V(G)$.
\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:admit-supply-assign-no-demand} ensures that the supply claimed by this configuration is satisfied completely inside $C^{+}$ while \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:admit-demand-used-sensibly} ensures that connections required from the outside are only used for a single solution tree.
\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:admit-every-F-uses-component-sensibly} is not necessary to ensure correctness, but to ensure that the number of admitted configurations stays singly exponential in ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
This is achieved by forbidding redundant configurations.
\par Later, we want to be able to only work with component-configurations and be able to ignore the underlying edge-disjoint trees that give rise to these configurations.
To this end, we need to know the set of configurations a component $C$ admits.
We call this its signature ${\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}$.
\par We now characterize, whether an instance is solvable solely based on the signatures of the available components.
For this, let $\Gamma$ be a function that assigns each component of $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ a configuration in ${\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}$.
We call $\Gamma$ a \emph{configuration selector}.
Not all configuration selectors are sensible, we only care about those that are \emph{valid}.
\par\begin{definition}We call $\Gamma$ \emph{valid}, if there is a function $\rho\colon 2^{S\cap V(G)}\times\N\to 2^{2^{S\cap V(G)}}$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@for all $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$, with $r_{U}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\left|\{(T,i)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}\times\N\mid U\in\rho(T,i)\}\right|}{{|\{(T,i)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}\times\N\mid U\in\rho(T,i)\}|}}{{|\{(T,i)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}\times\N\mid U\in\rho(T,i)\}|}}{{|\{(T,i)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}\times\N\mid U\in\rho(T,i)\}|}}}$ we have $$r_{U}+\sum_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}}{\mathchoice{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}}\leq\sum_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}}{\mathchoice{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}},$$\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $T\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, the hypergraph $(T\cup\bigcup\rho(T,i),\rho(T,i))$ is minimally connected,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ and $i\in[d(T)]$, denote with $$\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\{{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}\mid C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}},j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\}$$all subsets of $S$ that are connected for this terminal set.
Then, the hypergraph $\left((T\cap S)\cup\bigcup\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}\right)$ is connected.\end{enumerate}\end{definition}
\par In the definition above, the function $\rho$ is used to capture how the requirements of the terminal sets in $\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ are fulfilled.
That is, for a $T\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, $\rho(T,i)$ gives all connections that are needed to satisfy the $i$-th tree assigned to $T$ and \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-rho-connected} ensures that these connections actually connect $T$.
With \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-enough-supply} we ensure that there is enough supply to meet the demand.
Finally, \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-assign-connected} ensures that the explicitly stored solutions for the terminal sets $\mathcal{T}_{S}$ are actually connected.
\par We require minimal connectivity in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-rho-connected} to limit the number of needed variables in our ILP.
Note that we cannot require minimal connectivity in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-assign-connected} as this would prevent for any $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, $i\in d(T)$, and $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$ that ${\mathchoice{\left|{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}\right|}{{|{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}|}}}=1$, which is required in some instances.
\par We now show, that a valid configuration selector exists for an instance if and only if it is a positive instance.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $S$ be a nice fracture modulator of ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$.
Assume that \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance} is applied.
Then, the instance is positive if and only if there exists a valid configuration selector with respect to $S$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Assume that the instance is positive and let $(\mathcal{F},\pi)$ be a solution.
Assume without loss of generality that any $F\in\mathcal{F}$ is a tree such that its leaves are contained in $\pi(F)$.
We aim to define a valid configuration selector $\Gamma$.
Consider a component $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$.
We first define $\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}$, then $\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}$, and finally $\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}$.
\par Let $\mathcal{F}_{C}\subseteq\mathcal{F}$ be the trees that use edges of $C^{+}$.
We partition $\mathcal{F}_{C}$ by which terminal set a tree is associated with.
Let
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\mathcal{X}_{C}\coloneqq\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{C}\mid\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}_{C}\}$ be the trees that only have terminals in $C^{+}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathcal{Y}_{C}\coloneqq\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{C}\mid\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}_{S}\}$ be the trees that have terminals in multiple components of ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathcal{Z}_{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}_{C}\setminus(\mathcal{X}_{C}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{C})$ be the trees that do not have any terminal in $C$,
\end{itemize}
where we omit the component index if the component is clear from context.
\par Consider the graph $C^{+}$ and add for all $F\in\mathcal{X}$ a copy of $F[V(G)\setminus V(C^{+})]$ connected to the same vertices of $S$ as in $F$ to $C^{+}$.
Call the obtained graph $G_{C}$.
Contract all edges in $G_{C}$ that are not incident to $C^{+}$ to obtain $H_{C}$ (i.e., $H_{C}\coloneqq G_{C}/E(G_{C}-V(C^{+}))$).
This is exactly the graph we obtain when contracting all connected components in $G_{C}-V(C^{+})$ to a single vertex.
Since $S$ is a fracture modulator, the vertices in $V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})$ are only incident to vertices in $S$.
For all $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$, we set $${\mathchoice{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\left|\{v\in V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})\mid N_{H_{C}}(v)=U\}\right|}{{|\{v\in V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})\mid N_{H_{C}}(v)=U\}|}}{{|\{v\in V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})\mid N_{H_{C}}(v)=U\}|}}{{|\{v\in V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})\mid N_{H_{C}}(v)=U\}|}}}.$$
\par Consider any $F\in\mathcal{Z}$.
If we restrict our view to $F[V(C)]$, we notice that this graph might be disconnected.
So, we supply the sets of $S$ that are connected by each of these connected components to the other components, for which it is actually irrelevant these sets are connected via the same component.
Thus, for all $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$, we set $${\mathchoice{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}}\coloneqq\sum_{F\in\mathcal{Z}}{\mathchoice{\left|\{K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}\mid N_{F}(V(K))=U\}\right|}{{|\{K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}\mid N_{F}(V(K))=U\}|}}{{|\{K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}\mid N_{F}(V(K))=U\}|}}{{|\{K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}\mid N_{F}(V(K))=U\}|}}}.$$
\par To define $\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}$ and later $\rho$, we need a global numbering on the trees.
So, let for all $T\in\mathcal{T}$ the trees associated with this terminal let $\{F^{T}_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(T)$ be numbered globally.
Additionally, choose a surjection $\sigma_{C}\colon{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}\to C$ globally.
Let $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$, and $v\in C$ be given.
If $v\notin V(F_{i}^{T})$ or $i>d(T)$, for all $j\in\sigma^{-1}(v)$ we set ${\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}\coloneqq\emptyset$.
Otherwise, let $K$ be the connected component of $v$ in $F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})]$.
For all $j\in\sigma^{-1}(v)$ we set $${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}\coloneqq V(K)\cap S.$$
\par We now prove that $\Gamma$ defined as above, is a valid configuration selector.
For this, we first show that $\Gamma$ is a well-defined configuration selector, by showing that all components $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ admit $\Gamma(C)$.
\par Consider any $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, $j\in\sigma_{C}^{-1}(T\cap V(C))$, and let $v\coloneqq\sigma(j)$.
As $v\in T$, we have $v\in V(F^{T}_{i})$.
Additionally, there is a $C\neq C^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ with $V(C^{\prime})\cap T\neq\emptyset$.
Let $u\in T\cap V(C^{\prime})$ and consider the simple $vu$-path $P$ in $F^{T}_{i}$.
As $S$ is a fracture modulator, there is a first $s\in S$ on $P$.
Denote with $P^{\prime}$ the $vs$-subpath of $P$.
We have $V(P^{\prime})\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
So, the connected component of $v$ in $F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})]$ contains $s$ and by definition we have $s\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}$; so, ${\mathchoice{\assignOp\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}{\assignOp(T,i,j)}}\neq\emptyset$.
\par Next, we need to find a solution to ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C)\sigma)}}$.
We notice that the host graph of ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C)\sigma)}}$ is exactly $H_{C}$.
If there is a $T\in\mathcal{T}_{C}$ with $T\cap V(C)=\emptyset$, the vertex $\aug{T}$ is only connected to $S$ in ${G}^{\mathcal{T}}$ and so $V(C)=\{\aug{T}\lx@text@rbrace$, which means that $G[V(C)]$ is the empty graph as it does not contain any augmented vertices.
Thus, $\mathcal{F}_{C}$ is empty, which means $\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}$ and $\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}$ are the constant $0$-function.
Additionally all $v\in V(C)$ satisfy $v\notin V(G)\supseteq T$, so $\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}$ is the constant $\emptyset$-function.
Therefore, we can verify that in this case $C$ admits $\Gamma(C)$.
So, assume from now on, that such a $T$ does not exist.
\par Consider any tree $F\in\mathcal{X}$.
As $\aug{\pi(F)} \in V(C)$, we have $\pi(F)\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
Denote with $\phi\colon V(G_{C})\to V(H_{C})$ the mapping induced by the contractions of $E(G_{C}-V(C^{+}))$ in $G_{C}$.
Notice that for all $\{u,v\}\in E(G_{C})$, we have that either $\phi(u)=\phi(v)$, or $\phi(u)\phi(v)\in E(H_{C})$.
So, $\phi$ is almost a graph homomorphism.
Denote with $\phi_{E}\colon E(G_{C})\rightharpoonup E(H_{C})$ the partial edge mapping function induced by $\phi$.
Now let $\psi\colon G_{C}\to G$ be the graph homomorphism that maps each $v\in V(C^{+})$ to $v$ and all vertices in $V(G_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})$ to their original vertices in $G$.
Note that $\psi$ restricted to edges is an injection, as edges in $C^{+}$ are preserved and all other edges in $G_{C}$ correspond to edges in edge-disjoint trees of $\mathcal{X}$.
Thus, $F^{\prime}\coloneqq H_{C}[\phi_{E}(\psi^{-1}_{E}(F))]$ is a connected subgraph of $H_{C}$.
As $\aug{\pi(F)}\in V(C)$, we have $\pi(F)\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
Thus, $\phi(\psi^{-1}(\pi(F)))=\pi(F)$ and $F^{\prime}$ connects $\pi(F)$.
\par Consider any distinct $F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{X}$.
In creating $G_{C}$, we added different copies of $F_{1}[V(G)\setminus V(C^{+})]$ and $F_{2}[V(G)\setminus V(C^{+})]$, which means that $\phi_{E}(\psi_{E}^{-1}(F_{1}))$ and $\phi_{E}(\psi^{-1}_{E}(F_{2}))$ are disjoint.
So, the set $\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{H_{C}[\phi_{E}(\psi_{E}^{-1}(F))]\mid F\in\mathcal{X}\}$ contains edge-disjoint subgraphs of $H_{C}$.
As all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{C}$ satisfy $T\cap V(C)\neq\emptyset$, every tree assigned to $T$ must contain an edge of $C^{+}$ and is therefore contained in $\mathcal{F}_{C}$.
Thus, we can choose an assignment of $\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ to $T_{C}$ such that $d(T)$-many trees are assigned to each terminal set $T$.
\par Consider $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\coloneqq\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{Z}}\{F[N_{F}[V(K)]]\mid K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}\}$, that is for each component $K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C)]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}{\compOp(F[V(C)])}}$, the set $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$ contains the subgraph of $F$ induced on the vertices of $K$ and the vertices adjacent to $K$ in $F$.
Since every tree $F\in\mathcal{Z}$ is cycle-free, the trees induced by $F$ in $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$ do not share two or more vertices.
So, they also do not share any edges of $F$ and as the trees in $\mathcal{Z}$ are disjoint, this holds for $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$ as well.
Assigning an $F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$ to $S\cap V(F^{\prime})$, we get ${\mathchoice{\supplyOp\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}}$-many trees assigned to $U$ for each $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$.
\par Finally, let $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$.
We see that $\mathcal{K}^{T}_{i}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})]\right)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})])}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})])}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}[V(C^{+})])}}$ are vertex- and, thus, edge-disjoint trees.
By definition of $\assignOp$, we have $\{V(K)\}_{K\in K^{T}_{i}}={\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}$ and there is an assignment of $\mathcal{K}_{i}^{T}$ to ${\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}$.
Let $\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in d(T)}\{K^{T}_{i}\}$ be a set of edge-disjoint trees.
For all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, and $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}$, we assign a different tree $K\in\mathcal{K}$ with $V(K)=U$ to $U$.
So, to each $U\subseteq V(C^{+})$, we assign $\sum_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}}{\mathchoice{\left|\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\}\right|}{{|\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}{{|\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}{{|\{i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\mid U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}}$ distinct to trees $U$.
As $\bigcup_{K\in\mathcal{K}}E(K)\subseteq\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{Y}}E(F)$, the trees in $\mathcal{K}$ are edge-disjoint from the trees in $\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ and $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$.
So, $\mathcal{L}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{K}$ is a set of edge-disjoint trees that with the assignment $\pi$ described above solves ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C}\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C)\sigma_{C})}}$.
\par Now, we verify that $(\mathcal{L},\pi)$ not only solves ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C}\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C)\sigma_{C})}}$, but $(\sigma_{C},\mathcal{L},\pi)$ actually gives rise to $\Gamma(C)$ on $C$.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Recall that $\mathcal{S}=\{X\subseteq S\cap V(G)\mid{\mathchoice{\supplyOp\left(X\right)}{\supplyOp(X)}{\supplyOp(X)}{\supplyOp(X)}}>0\}$.
By choice of $\pi$, $\mathcal{Z^{\prime}}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{S})$ and all $F\in\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}$ fulfill $V(F)\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Consider some $v\in V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})$, denote with $F\in\mathcal{X}$ the tree that created the vertices $\phi^{-1}(v)$, and let $F^{\prime}\coloneqq H_{C}[\phi_{E}(\psi^{-1}_{E}(F))]\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ denote the corresponding tree in $\mathcal{L}$.
Since all edges between $\phi^{-1}(v)$ and $N_{G_{C}}(\phi^{-1}(v))$ are contained in $F$, all edges between $v$ and $N_{H_{C}}(v)$ are contained in $F^{\prime}$.
Thus, ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{F^{\prime}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{F^{\prime}}(v)}{\degOp_{F^{\prime}}(v)}{\degOp_{F^{\prime}}(v)}}={\mathchoice{\degOp_{H_{C}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}}$.
Since all trees in $\mathcal{L}$ are edge-disjoint, this implies that no other $F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}$ fulfills $v\in V(F^{\prime\prime})$.
\par We now show that ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{H_{C}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}}\geq 2$.
Consider any $u\in\phi^{-1}(v)\subseteq V(F)$.
We know $\aug{\pi(F)} \in V(C)$, so $\pi(F)\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
Since $u\notin V(C^{+})$, we have $u\notin\pi(F)$, and, by assumption, $u$ is not a leaf of $F$.
Let $x,y\in V(C^{+})$ be leaves of $F$ such that $u$ is contained in the simple path $P$ connecting $x$ to $y$ in $F$.
Since $x,y\in V(C^{+})$, $v=\phi(u)$ is neither $x=\phi(x)$ nor $y=\phi(y)$.
Let $a$ denote the last vertex preceding any $\phi^{-1}(v)$, and let $b$ denote the first vertex following any $\phi^{-1}(v)$ in $P$.
Since $S$ is a fracture modulator, $a,b\in S$.
Therefore, $v$ is connected to $a$ as well as $b$, and we have ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{H_{C}}\left(v\right)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}{\degOp_{H_{C}}(v)}}\geq 2$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Consider $F\in\mathcal{L}$.
By the definitions of the terminal sets, we have ${\mathchoice{\left|\pi(F)\right|}{{|\pi(F)|}}{{|\pi(F)|}}{{|\pi(F)|}}}\geq 2$; so, all vertices in $V(F)$ have an incident edge in $F$.
Additionally, it is ensured that $V(F)\cap V(C)$ is non-empty, showing that $E(F)\cap E(C^{+})\neq\emptyset$.
As the trees in $\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{K}$ are subgraphs of trees in $\mathcal{F}$, they are cycle-free.
Let $F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ and let $F\in\mathcal{X}$ be such that $H_{C}[\phi_{E}(\psi^{-1}_{E}(F))]=F^{\prime}$.
Note that $F$ is a tree.
Since $F$ is isomorphic to $G_{C}[\psi^{-1}_{E}(F)]$, both are cycle-free.
Observe that $F^{\prime}=H_{C}[\phi_{E}(\psi^{-1}_{E}(F))]$ is equal to $G_{C}[\psi^{-1}_{E}(F)]$ with some edges contracted; so, $F^{\prime}$ is cycle-free as well.
\end{enumerate}
\par We have proven that $\Gamma$ is a configuration selector.
Next, we prove that $\Gamma$ is valid.
For this we need to specify a function $\rho\colon 2^{S\cap V(G)}\times\N\to 2^{2^{S\cap V(G)}}$ that specifies how, the terminal sets in $\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ are fulfilled.
For any $T\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, consider $F^{T}_{i}-S$.
This graph might be disconnected and each component connects some subset of $S$.
So, we set $\rho(T,i)=\{N_{F^{T}_{i}}(V(K))\mid K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F^{T}_{i}-S\right)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}}\}$ as each subset of $S$ that gets connected by one of those components.
All other values of $\rho$ we define to be $\emptyset$.
Now, we verify the properties that are required for validity.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Consider any $U\subseteq S\cap V(G)$.
First, we notice that for all $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, we have
\@@amsalign
Now, consider
\@@amsalign
Denote with $e(F)$ the inner term of the sum over $F\in\mathcal{F}$ and denote with $s(F)$ and $d(F)$ the positive and negative sums in $e(F)$, respectively.
We now prove that $$e(F)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}\land\exists K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F-S\right)}{\compOp(F-S)}{\compOp(F-S)}{\compOp(F-S)}}:N_{F}(V(K))=U\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}$$\par For this, we first show that for all $F\in\mathcal{F}$ with $\pi(F)\notin\mathcal{T}^{\star}$, it holds that $e(F)=0$.
We distinguish two cases.
First, let $\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$.
For all $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, we have $F\in\mathcal{Y}_{C}$; so, $s(F)=d(F)=e(F)=0$.
Now let $\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}\setminus(\mathcal{T}_{S}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\star})$.
Denote with $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ the unique component with $\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}_{C}$.
Now assume there is a $K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F-V(C^{+})\right)}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}}$ with $N_{F}(V(K))=U$.
As $V(K)$ has at least 2 incident outgoing edges in $F$ and since $F$ is cycle-free, this $K$ is unique and $d(F)=1$.
Since $S$ is a fracture modulator, there is a $C^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ with $V(K)\subseteq V(C^{\prime})$.
This is also the unique component $C^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ which has a $K^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[V(C^{\prime\prime})]\right)}{\compOp(F[V(C^{\prime\prime})])}{\compOp(F[V(C^{\prime\prime})])}{\compOp(F[V(C^{\prime\prime})])}}$ with $N_{F}(V(K))=U$.
Thus, $s(F)=1$ and $e(F)=0$.
If there is no $K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F-V(C^{+})\right)}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}{\compOp(F-V(C^{+}))}}$ with $N_{F}(V(K))=U$, we have that $d(F)=s(F)=e(F)=0$.
\par Now consider $F\in\mathcal{F}$ with $\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$.
And let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ be the unique component with $\pi(F)\in\mathcal{T}_{C}$.
Notice that $\aug{F}$ is only connected to $S$ and so $C=\{\aug{F}\lx@text@rbrace$.
Thus, $\mathcal{F}_{C}=\emptyset$ and, in particular, $\mathcal{X}_{C}=\emptyset$.
Therefore, $d(F)=0$.
Now, assume there is a $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ such that there is a $K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F[C]\right)}{\compOp(F[C])}{\compOp(F[C])}{\compOp(F[C])}}$ with $N_{F}(V(K))=U$.
With the same argument as above, $C$ is unique.
Thus, $s(F)=1$.
Finally, notice that the existence of the pair $(C,K)$ is equivalent to the existence of a $K^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F-S\right)}{\compOp(F-S)}{\compOp(F-S)}{\compOp(F-S)}}$ with $N_{F}(V(K^{\prime}))=U$, which proves that $e(F)$ has the desired alternative representation and so
\@@amsalign
\par Now, we consider $r_{U}$.
We observe that
\@@amsalign
and so $r_{U}+\sum_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}}{\mathchoice{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\demandOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}}=\sum_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}}{\mathchoice{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}{\supplyOp_{\Gamma(C)}(U)}}.$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@ First we prove that the considered hypergraph is connected. Then, we show that it is minimally connected.
\par Let $T\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$ be given.
Consider $s,t\in T\cup\bigcup\rho(T,i)$.
We notice that $u,v\in V(F^{T}_{i})$, so there is a $uv$-path $P$ in $F^{T}_{i}$.
Denote with $s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{k}$ the sequence of vertices in $S\cap V(G)$ of $P$.
As $G[S]$ is edgeless, for all $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[k-1\right]}{[k-1]}{[k-1]}{[k-1]}}$ the vertices $s_{i}$ and $s_{i+1}$ are not adjacent in $P$.
So, there is a the sub-path connecting $s_{i}$ and $s_{i+1}$, whose inner vertices are contained in one component of $F^{T}_{i}-S$.
Therefore, there is a subset in $\rho(T,i)$ containing both $s_{i}$ and $s_{i+1}$ and the hypergraph $(T\cup\bigcup\rho(T,i),\rho(T,i))$ is connected.
\par Now, assume there is an $R\in\rho(T,i)$ such that the hypergraph $H\coloneqq(T\cup\bigcup\rho(T,i),\allowbreak\rho(T,i)\setminus\{R\})$ is connected.
Let $K\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left(F^{T}_{i}-S\right)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}{\compOp(F^{T}_{i}-S)}}$ with $N_{F^{T}_{i}}(V(K))=R$ be chosen.
Since $H$ is connected, by a similar argument as above, $F^{T}_{i}-K$ is connected as well.
We assumed that the leaves $L$ of $F^{T}_{i}$, fulfill $L\subseteq\pi(F^{T}_{i})=T\subseteq S$.
So, ${\mathchoice{\left|R\right|}{{|R|}}{{|R|}}{{|R|}}}\geq 2$ and let $s,t\in R$ be distinct.
Observe that there are $st$-paths in $F^{T}_{i}-K$ and $F^{T}_{i}[V(K^{+})]$ which are edge-disjoint.
Thus, $F^{T}_{i}$ is not cycle-free, which we assumed.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@This follows analogously to the proof of connectivity in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:proof-equiv-selector-positive-valid-terminals-in-S} and concludes the proof that $\Gamma$ is a valid configuration selector.
\end{enumerate}
\par Now, assume that there is a valid configuration selector $\Gamma$.
For every $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, let $(\sigma_{C},\mathcal{F}_{C},\pi_{C})$ give rise to $\Gamma(C)$ on $C$ and denote the host graph of ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C}\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C),\sigma_{C})}{\confInstOp(C,\Gamma(C)\sigma_{C})}}$ with $H_{C}$.
We now prove that the instance is indeed solvable by constructing a solution.
For this, we split $\mathcal{F}_{C}$ into parts according to the designated purpose each tree fulfills in our solution.
\par First, we set $\mathcal{S}_{C}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}_{C}(\mathcal{T}^{\star})$ to be all trees that $\pi_{C}$ assigns to a terminal set contained in $S$; so, it will be used to provide connections of subsets of $S$ to other trees.
The remaining trees of $\mathcal{F}_{C}$ will either directly contribute to some $\mathcal{T}_{C}$, or will be used to fulfill some requirement of a terminal set in $\mathcal{T}_{S}$.
\par Let $U\subseteq V(C^{+})$ with $U\cap V(C)\neq\emptyset$.
For each $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$ with $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma_{C}\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma_{C})}}$ choose a distinct $A^{C}_{T,i,U}$ from $\pi^{-1}_{C}(U)$, which will be used to construct the $i$-th tree for the terminal set $T$.
Finally, we set $\mathcal{R}_{U}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}_{C}(U)\setminus\{A_{T,i,U}^{C}\mid T\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}\}$ to be the trees assigned to the terminal set $U$ in the final solution.
As trees in $\mathcal{F}_{C}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{C}$ might contain edges and vertices not present in $G$, we cannot directly include them into our final solution.
\par To fix this problem, consider for each $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ the set $V(H_{C})\setminus V(C^{+})$, assume they are all disjoint and call their union $K$.
Let $\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\bigcup_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}}\mathcal{S}_{C}$ and choose an injection $\eta\colon K\to\mathcal{S}$ such that for all $v\in K$ where $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ is such that $v\in V(H_{C})$, we have that $\pi_{C}(\eta(v))=N(v)$.
The existence of $\eta$ is guaranteed by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-enough-supply} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:valid-selector}.
Note that $\mathcal{S}$ is a set of edge-disjoint trees.
\par This function gives us a way to consider a vertex in $K$ and find a unique replacement tree for it using edges and vertices in $G$ that preserve connectivity.
Additionally, consider a $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ and distinct $F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{C}$ if we replace the vertices $v\in(V(F_{1})\cup V(F_{2}))\cap K$ by $\eta(v)$ in the respective trees, they are still edge-disjoint since $V(F_{1})\cap K$ and $V(F_{2})\cap K$ are disjoint and so the vertices get replaced by different trees.
\par Let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ and $T\in\mathcal{T}_{C}$ be such that $T\cap V(C)\neq\emptyset$.
Consider any $F\in\mathcal{R}_{T}$ and denote with $F^{*}$ the graph obtained by replacing every vertex in $v\in V(F)\cap K$ with $\eta(v)$.
Note that $F^{*}$ only uses vertices and edges of $G$.
Let $\mathcal{R}_{T}^{*}\coloneqq\{F^{*}\mid F\in\mathcal{R}_{T}\}$ and assign all those edge-disjoint subgraphs of $G$ to the terminal set $T$ in the final solution.
Noticing that $${\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{R}_{T}^{*}\right|}{{|\mathcal{R}_{T}^{*}|}}{{|\mathcal{R}_{T}^{*}|}}{{|\mathcal{R}_{T}^{*}|}}}=d^{\prime}(T)-{\mathchoice{\left|\{U\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(U)\right]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}}\mid T\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(U,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}}\}\right|}{{|\{U\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(U)\right]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}}\mid T\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(U,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}{{|\{U\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(U)\right]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}}\mid T\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(U,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}{{|\{U\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(U)\right]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}{[d(U)]}}\mid T\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(U,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(U,i,\sigma)}}\}|}}}=d(T),$$ we can now turn our attention to the other terminal sets.
\par First, we consider $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$ and let $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$.
Denote the union of all subgraphs that are selected for this terminal set to satisfy the $i$-th tree with $$F=\bigcup_{C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}},U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp(T,i,\sigma)}}}A_{T,i,U}^{C}.$$
We now prove that $F$ is connected and that $T\subseteq V(F)$.
\par Consider $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$, and $s,t\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}\subseteq S$.
We first prove that $s$ can reach $t$ via $F$.
Any vertex in $\sigma^{-1}(j)$ gives rise to an $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,\sigma_{C}\right)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}}\subseteq V(C^{+})$ with $s,t\in U$.
The graph $A^{C}_{T,i,U}$ is connected, a subgraph of $F$, and $s,t\in V(A^{C}_{T,i,U})$; so, $s$ can reach $t$ via $A^{C}_{T,i,U}$ and $F$ as well.
\par Consider $s,t\in V(F)\cap S$.
We now show that $s$ can reach $t$ via $F$.
We know by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-assign-connected} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:valid-selector}, that there is a sequence of $(C_{1},j_{1}),(C_{2},j_{2}),\dots,(C_{k},j_{k})$ such that $s\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{1})}\left(T,i,j_{1}\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{1})}(T,i,j_{1})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{1})}(T,i,j_{1})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{1})}(T,i,j_{1})}}$, $t\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{k})}\left(T,i,j_{k}\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{k})}(T,i,j_{k})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{k})}(T,i,j_{k})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{k})}(T,i,j_{k})}}$, and for all $\ell\in{\mathchoice{\left[k-1\right]}{[k-1]}{[k-1]}{[k-1]}}$ the sets ${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{i})}\left(T,i,j_{i}\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{i})}(T,i,j_{i})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{i})}(T,i,j_{i})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{i})}(T,i,j_{i})}}$ and ${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell+1})}\left(T,i,j_{\ell+1}\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell+1})}(T,i,j_{\ell+1})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell+1})}(T,i,j_{\ell+1})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell+1})}(T,i,j_{\ell+1})}}$ overlap.
As for all $\ell\in{\mathchoice{\left[k\right]}{[k]}{[k]}{[k]}}$, the sets\allowbreak{} ${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell})}\left(T,i,j_{\ell}\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell})}(T,i,j_{\ell})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell})}(T,i,j_{\ell})}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C_{\ell})}(T,i,j_{\ell})}}$ are connected via $F$, there is a path from $s$ to $t$ via $F$.
\par To show, that $F$ is connected, it is now enough to show for every $u\in V(F)\setminus S$, that there is a path to some vertex in $V(F)\cap S$ using edges of $F$.
As $u\in V(F)$, there exists $C$ and $i$ such that there is an $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,\sigma_{C}\right)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}}$ with $u\in U$.
By definition, there is an $s\in U\cap S$.
Since $A^{C}_{T,i,U}$ is connected, a subgraph of $F$, and contains $u$ and $s$, the vertex $u$ can reach $s$ in $F$.
\par To show that $T\subseteq V(F)$, first let $t\in T\cap S$.
Consider \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-assign-connected} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:valid-selector}, and let $H$ denote the considered hypergraph.
If $\{t\}=V(H)$, let $t^{\prime}\in T\setminus S$—which exists since $T\not\subseteq S$—and $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ be the component containing $t^{\prime}$.
Then, for all $j\in\sigma^{-1}_{C}(t^{\prime})$, we have ${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}=\{t\}$.
Otherwise, since $H$ is connected, there is a hyperedge incident to $t$ in $H$.
So, there are, by definition, $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ and $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$ with $t\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}$.
In either case, there is a $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ and $j\in{\mathchoice{\left[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}{[{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}]}}$ with $t\in{\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}$.
Now, since $\sigma(j)$ is a assigned to some set containing $t$, there is a $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,\sigma\right)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma)}}$ with $t\in U\subseteq V(C^{+})$.
So, $t\in V(A^{C}_{T,i,U})\subseteq V(F)$.
Now, consider $t\in T\setminus S$.
Let $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ be the component containing $t$ and let $j\in\sigma^{-1}_{C}(t)$.
By \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:admit-terminal-set-assigned} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:admit-configuration}, we know that ${\mathchoice{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,j\right)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}{\assignOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,j)}}\neq\emptyset$, so there is an $U\in{\mathchoice{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}\left(T,i,\sigma_{C}\right)}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}{\assignSetsOp_{\Gamma(C)}(T,i,\sigma_{C})}}$ with $t\in U$.
Since $U\subseteq V(A^{C}_{T,i,U})\subseteq V(F)$, we have $T\subseteq V(F)$.
\par As $F$ is not a subgraph of $G$, we cannot assign it to the terminal set $T$ in our solution.
Now consider the graph $F^{*}$ obtained from $F$ by replacing all $v\in V(F)\setminus V(G)$ by the subgraph $\eta(v)$.
This is a connected subgraph of $G$ and, as we only removed vertices not contained in $V(G)$, we have $T\subseteq V(F^{*})$.
We assign $F^{*}$ to $T$.
Note that all $F^{*}$ created in this manner are edge-disjoint from each other and the subgraphs already assigned to other terminal sets and that to each $T\in\mathcal{T}_{S}$, we assign $d(T)$-many such subgraphs.
\par To construct the subgraphs assigned to the terminal sets $\mathcal{T}^{\star}$, let $\rho$ be a function witnessing validity of $\Gamma$ on $G$.
Let $L\coloneqq\{(T,i,U)\mid T\in\mathcal{T}_{S},i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}},U\in\rho(T,i)\}$.
Choose an injection $\mu\colon L\to\mathcal{S}$ such that ${\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(\mu\right)}{\imageOp(\mu)}{\imageOp(\mu)}{\imageOp(\mu)}}$ and ${\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(\eta\right)}{\imageOp(\eta)}{\imageOp(\eta)}{\imageOp(\eta)}}$ are disjoint, and for all $(T,i,U)\in L$ with $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ denoting the component containing $\mu(T,i,U)$, we have $\pi_{C}(\mu(T,i,U))=U$.
The existence of $\mu$ is guaranteed by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-enough-supply} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:valid-selector}.
\par Now let $T\in\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and $i\in{\mathchoice{\left[d(T)\right]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}{[d(T)]}}$, consider $F\coloneqq\bigcup_{U\in\rho(T,i)}\mu(T,i,U)$, and let the corresponding hypergraph considered in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:valid-selector-rho-connected} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:valid-selector} be denoted by $H$.
As we assume the instance to be reduced by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance}, which in turn applies \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:sensible-terminal-sets} exhaustively, we have ${\mathchoice{\left|T\right|}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}{{|T|}}}\geq 2$.
As $T\subseteq V(H)$ and as $H$ is connected, for every $t\in T$, there is an $U\in E(H)=\rho(T,i)$ with $t\in U$ and so $t\in V(F)$.
Additionally, as $H$ is connected, so is $F$.
We now assign $F$ to $T$ in our final solution.
Note that by the injectivity of $\mu$ and the discontinues of ${\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(\mu\right)}{\imageOp(\mu)}{\imageOp(\mu)}{\imageOp(\mu)}}$ and ${\mathchoice{\imageOp\left(\eta\right)}{\imageOp(\eta)}{\imageOp(\eta)}{\imageOp(\eta)}}$, all these subgraphs are edge-disjoint from each other and all previously assigned subgraphs.
Additionally, we assign $d(T)$ subgraphs to $T$.
Thus, our final solution solves the instance.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Signatures and Equivalence Classes}
When building our ILP, we want to treat components with the same signature equally.
More specifically, we want to represent all components that share the same signature by a common set of variables.
This Section has three goals.
First, we analyze the equivalence relation on ${\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ induced by whether or not two components have the same signature.
More specifically, we bound the number of non-empty equivalence classes.
Second, we bound the size of the signatures.
Third, we present a simple method to compute the signature of a component.
\par To analyze the number of non-empty equivalence classes, we provide a sufficient condition on when two components are equivalent.
We choose this condition in such a way, that it is almost mechanical to bound the number of non-empty equivalence classes for a given instance.
\par\begin{definition}Let $C_{1},C_{2}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, we call $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ \emph{indistinguishable}, if there exists a graph isomorphism $\phi:C^{+}_{1}\to C^{+}_{2}$ such that
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\phi\big|_{S}={\mathchoice{\identityOp\left(S\right)}{\identityOp(S)}{\identityOp(S)}{\identityOp(S)}}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $v\in V(C^{+})$, for all $v\in V(C^{+}_{1})$, we have $v\in V(G)$ if and only if $\phi(v)\in V(G)$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all $T\in\mathcal{T}_{V(C_{1})}$, we have $d(T)=d({\mathchoice{\augOp^{-1}\left(\phi(\aug{T})\right)}{\augOp_{}^{-1}(\phi(\aug{T}))}{\augOp_{}^{-1}(\phi(\aug{T}))}{\augOp_{}^{-1}(\phi(\aug{T}))}})$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
\par We call the equivalence classes induced by whether or not components are indistinguishable by the name \emph{indistinguishablility classes}.
First, we show that if two components are indistinguishable, they are in fact equivalent as well.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C,C^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ be indistinguishable components.
Then, $C$ and $C^{\prime}$ are equivalent (i.e., ${\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}={\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C^{\prime}\right)}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}}$).
\end{lemma}
\@proof Consider any $\gamma\in{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}$.
As indistinguishablility is a symmetric property, it is enough to show $\gamma\in{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C^{\prime}\right)}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}{\signatureOp(C^{\prime})}}$ to prove the claim.
\par Let $(\sigma,\mathcal{F},\pi)$ give rise to $\gamma$ on $C$ and let $\phi$ be an isomorphism between $C$ and $C^{\prime}$ satisfying the additional requirements of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:indistinguishable-components}.
Since for all $v\in S$, we have $v=\phi(v)$ and for all $u\in V(C^{+})$, we have $u\in V(G)$ if and only if $\phi(u)\in V(G)$, it is possible to extend $\phi\big|_{V(C^{+})\cap V(G)}$ to an isomorphism $\psi$ between the host-graphs of ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$ and ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C^{\prime},\gamma,\sigma\circ\phi^{-1}\right)}{\confInstOp(C^{\prime},\gamma,\sigma\circ\phi^{-1})}{\confInstOp(C^{\prime},\gamma,\sigma\circ\phi^{-1})}{\confInstOp(C^{\prime},\gamma\sigma\circ\phi^{-1})}}$ such that $\phi\big|_{V(C^{+})\cap V(G)}=\psi\big|_{V(C^{+})\cap V(G)}$.
One can now verify that $$\left(\sigma\circ\phi^{-1},\left\{\psi_{E}(F)\mid F\in\mathcal{F}\right\},\psi\circ\pi\circ\psi^{-1}\right)$$ gives rise to $\gamma$ on $C^{\prime}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par We now aim to bound the number of non-empty equivalence classes.
To this end, we bound the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes, which is an upper bound on the number of equivalence classes.
We only consider instances, where \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{rr:degree-negative-instance} was applied.
Note that, if we did not apply this reduction rule, we could exploit \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:same-demand} of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{def:indistinguishable-components} to create a family of instances, where the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes is not bounded by any function of ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\par\begin{lemma}There are at most $2^{{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}}$ non-empty indistinguishablility classes on ${\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof First, we bound the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes whose components only contain augmented vertices.
As augmented vertices are not adjacent to each other, every component that only contains augmented vertices, contains exactly one such vertex and no non-augmented vertices.
For every terminal set we introduce exactly one augmented vertex.
So, for each pair of augmented vertices $u,v$ with $N(u)=N(v)$, we have that $u=v$.
Thus, for each $R\subseteq S$, there is at most one augmented vertex $v$ with $N(v)=R$ and at most one component $C^{\prime}\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ with $N(V(C^{\prime}))=R$.
Therefore, there are no more than $2^{{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}$ non-empty indistinguishablility classes that only contain augmented vertices.
\par Now, we bound the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes whose component contains at least one non-augmented vertex.
For each component $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, the graph $C^{+}$ has at most ${\mathchoice{\OOp\left(|S|^{2}\right)}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}}$ potential edges.
So, there are at most $2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left(|S|^{2}\right)}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}{\OOp(|S|^{2})}}$ non-isomorphic graphs for each of the $|S|$ different sizes of the components.
Overall, there are at most ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}=2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}$ non-isomorphic $C^{+}$.
According to \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:same-augmented}, the isomorphism must preserve whether or not a vertex is augmented.
This increases the number of possible non-empty indistinguishablility classes by a factor of at most $2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\par Finally, we need to bound the impact of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:same-demand}.
Consider any augmented vertex $a\in V(C^{+})$.
If $a\in S$, \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:same-demand} is satisfied by every isomorphism $\psi$ that satisfies \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{item:modulator-identity}.
So, assume $a\in V(C)$.
As augmented vertices do not share adjacent edges, $a$ needs to be adjacent to some non-augmented vertex $v\in V(C)$.
So, ${\mathchoice{\augOp^{-1}\left(a\right)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}}\notin\mathcal{T}^{\star}$ and by \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{stmt:terminal-sets-few-requirements}, we have that $d({\mathchoice{\augOp^{-1}\left(a\right)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}{\augOp^{-1}(a)}})\leq 2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
Therefore, every augmented vertex is in one of $2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$ states.
There are at most ${\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$ many such augmented vertices.
Thus, the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes is increased by a factor of ${(2{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}=2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}}$.
Overall the number of non-empty indistinguishablility classes is bounded by $2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\log{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}})}}=2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par Using \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{stmt:indist-classes-preserve-signature}, we conclude that this bound also applies to equivalence classes.
\par\begin{corollary}On ${\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$ there are at most $2^{{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{2})}}}$ non-empty equivalence classes.
\end{corollary}
\par Now, we continue this Section by bounding the size of signatures that are admitted by any component.
For this, we consider a necessary condition for a component configuration to be admitted by any component.
\par\begin{definition}Let $\gamma$ be a component-configuration.
We call $\gamma$ viable, if $$\sum_{U\subseteq S\cap V(G)}{\mathchoice{\demandOp\left(U\right)}{\demandOp(U)}{\demandOp(U)}{\demandOp(U)}}\leq u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\text{ and }\sum_{U\subseteq S\cap V(G)}{\mathchoice{\supplyOp\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}}\leq u.$$The set of all viable configurations is denoted by $\allViable$.
\end{definition}
\par We now show, that any configuration, that is admitted by a component is indeed viable.
\par\begin{lemma}For all $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, we have ${\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}\subseteq\allViable$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $\gamma\in{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}$, let $(\sigma,\mathcal{F},\pi)$ give rise to $\gamma$ on $C$, and let $H$ be the host-graph of ${\mathchoice{\confInstOp\left(C,\gamma,\sigma\right)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma,\sigma)}{\confInstOp(C,\gamma\sigma)}}$.
For any $F\in\mathcal{F}$, every vertex $x\in V(F)\setminus V(G)$ has $N(x)\subseteq S$ and ${\mathchoice{\degOp_{F}\left(x\right)}{\degOp_{F}(x)}{\degOp_{F}(x)}{\degOp_{F}(x)}}\geq 2$.
Since $F$ is cycle-free, the number of such vertices is bounded by $|S|$.
Additionally, every vertex in $V(H)\setminus V(G)$ is contained in some tree of $\mathcal{F}$.
Therefore ${\mathchoice{\left|V(H)\setminus V(G)\right|}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{F}\right|}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}}$.
Since every tree of $\mathcal{F}$ contains an edge from $E(G[C^{+}])$ of which there are at most $u$, and since each such edge is contained in at most one tree of $\mathcal{F}$, we have ${\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{F}\right|}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}}\leq u$.
Thus, ${\mathchoice{\left|V(H)\setminus V(G)\right|}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}{{|V(H)\setminus V(G)|}}}=\sum_{U\subseteq S\cap V(G)}{\mathchoice{\demandOp\left(U\right)}{\demandOp(U)}{\demandOp(U)}{\demandOp(U)}}\leq u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}$.
\par Furthermore, $\sum_{U\subseteq S\cap V(G)}{\mathchoice{\supplyOp\left(U\right)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}{\supplyOp(U)}}=\sum_{U\subseteq S\cap V(G)}d^{\prime}(U)\leq{\mathchoice{\left|\mathcal{F}\right|}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}{{|\mathcal{F}|}}}\leq u$.
\qed\end@proof
\par Now, we count the number of viable configurations. This gives an upper bound on the size of any signature.
\par\begin{lemma}For all $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, we have ${\mathchoice{\left|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}\right|}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|\allViable\right|}{{|\allViable|}}{{|\allViable|}}{{|\allViable|}}}\leq 2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}}$.
The set $\allViable$ can be enumerated in running time $2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof For all $C\in{\mathchoice{\compOp\left({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S\right)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}{\compOp({G}^{\mathcal{T}}-S)}}$, from \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{stmt:admitted-configuration-is-viable} it follows that ${\mathchoice{\left|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}\right|}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}{{|{\mathchoice{\signatureOp\left(C\right)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}{\signatureOp(C)}}|}}}\leq{\mathchoice{\left|\!\allViable\!\right|}{{|\!\allViable\!|}}{{|\!\allViable\!|}}{{|\!\allViable\!|}}}$.
The number of different $\demandOp$ and $\supplyOp$ functions in viable configurations is $\binom{2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}{u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}\leq\left(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}\right)^{u{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}}=2^{{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}}}$ and $\binom{2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+u}{u}\leq\left(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}+u\right)^{u}=2^{{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{3}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{3})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{3})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{3})}}}$ respectively.
They can be enumerated in time ${\mathchoice{\OOp\left(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}}\right)}{\OOp(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}})}{\OOp(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}})}{\OOp(2^{\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}})}}=2^{\mathchoice{\OOp\left({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4}\right)}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}{\OOp({\mathchoice{\left|S\right|}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}{{|S|}}}^{4})}}$ and
